15 Sustainable AI

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Fleet stack focus for this chapter.

Why do power limits determine what machine learning systems can exist, not just what they cost to operate?

Power is an operational expense and a physical constraint on what can be built. A data center has a power budget determined by electrical infrastructure and cooling capacity; a training run that exceeds it cannot proceed at the intended scale regardless of its financial budget. Adding accelerators only helps if the site can supply their electricity and remove their heat. A design that runs efficiently on one machine can therefore become infeasible when replicated across a fleet. The consequences reach beyond a delayed training run. Power reserved for training may be unavailable to serving, while equipment kept ready for failures consumes resources even when it performs no useful work. Moving work to another location can relieve one constraint while changing its emissions, access to data, or ability to meet deadlines. Efficiency improves these choices, but lower energy per request does not ensure lower total consumption when demand grows. Nor does a clean electricity supply erase the environmental cost of manufacturing and replacing equipment. Engineers must judge feasible designs over their useful lives and account for the communities sharing their water and electricity supplies. Sustainability therefore belongs alongside accuracy, latency, and reliability in system design: a fleet must deliver useful service within resource limits that its performance targets cannot override. In C³ terms, compute, communication, and coordination draw on a shared physical budget, so their combined resource demand determines whether the fleet can operate at all.

Learning Objectives
  • Explain why facility power and cooling limits constrain which ML systems can physically exist
  • Analyze how scaling-driven compute growth, hardware efficiency, and demand rebound shape fleet resource demand
  • Calculate operational, embodied, and lifecycle carbon from energy use, power usage effectiveness, manufacturing, and grid intensity
  • Analyze how computation, memory movement, power delivery, cooling, and water availability constrain sustainable fleet operation
  • Compare training, inference, edge, and federated workloads by energy, carbon, and battery budgets
  • Design mitigation strategies across algorithms, infrastructure, scheduling, hardware lifetime, and carbon-aware operations
  • Evaluate sustainability claims against a measurement contract that fixes functional unit, system boundary, and carbon signal
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

15.1 The Energy Ceiling

The robustness of Robust AI was bought in compute, and every operation that compute performs draws power and sheds heat. A model can be secure against adversaries and robust under distribution shift yet still fail if the available grid cannot power its accelerators or the facility cannot cool them. Sustainability begins at that boundary, where accuracy, latency, and reliability are no longer enough because the system must also fit within power, cooling, water, carbon, and hardware lifetime budgets. Every operation a model performs is paid for in energy, and power density sets the ceiling on data-center computational capacity.1

1 Joule energy scale: An A100 modeled at its rated thermal design power consumes 400 J every second (400 W); actual workload power requires measurement. Large-model training has no single defensible joule range: model size, hardware count, realized power, utilization, and run duration can each shift total energy by orders of magnitude. The engineering lesson lies in the conversion from repeated per-operation costs to facility energy, so this chapter reports measured or modeled workload energy rather than assigning every large run to one fixed range.

When an engineer optimizes a database query to save 100 ms, that is ordinary performance tuning. At fleet scale, the same saving repeated billions of times per day can reduce electrical demand, cooling load, and carbon emissions if it reduces measured energy per completed query (Lacoste et al. 2019). A system that exceeds its operating envelope has failed operationally in the same sense as one that crashes, because it cannot be deployed at the intended scale.

That ceiling makes sustainability a design constraint, not a reporting category. The same engineering discipline that budgets memory, bandwidth, and fault tolerance must also budget joules, cooling load, embodied carbon, and hardware replacement cycles.

Physical feasibility establishes which fleets can run. Sustainability then compares the environmental cost of feasible designs over their useful lives. The account starts from workload energy at a declared measurement boundary and extends to carbon, water, and manufacturing. The dominant lifecycle term then decides among efficiency, placement, and hardware reuse. Communication and coordination belong in that account alongside compute, because transfers, synchronization stalls, and recovery keep equipment powered even when useful computation stops. Under the Fleet Law, a distributed step costs its average fleet power multiplied by its step time, so every second of exposed communication or synchronization is also a block of joules. Equation 13 writes that account out where the chapter turns to distributed training.

Contemporary machine learning applications operate at industrial scale, with individual campuses drawing power at the level of heavy industrial plants. Training a single large AI model can consume as much electricity as roughly 120 US homes do in an entire year. The scaling laws that motivate fleet-scale training (principle 9) price each further loss reduction in additional compute, data, and parameters, so compute demand has grown faster than hardware efficiency (Sevilla et al. 2022). That scaling-driven growth is the first way demand outruns efficiency, and sustainable AI treats it as a first-class systems problem rather than an externality to be managed after deployment.

↩︎ The power-law scaling of loss with compute, data, and parameters, and the compute-optimal allocation that follows from it, are established in AI Scaling Laws.

Definition 15.1: Sustainable AI

Sustainable AI is the systems engineering practice of measuring and optimizing the full environmental cost of ML systems (energy, water, and embodied carbon across training, inference, and hardware manufacturing) and incorporating those costs as explicit constraints in architecture decisions alongside performance and accuracy objectives (Wynsberghe 2021; Lannelongue et al. 2021; Henderson et al. 2020).

  1. Significance: Training GPT-3 consumed approximately 1,287 MWh of energy (Li 2020), equivalent to roughly 120 US household-years of electricity. The same lifecycle logic applies after training: pretrained models can often be adapted with far less work than training from scratch, and high-volume inference can eventually dominate total energy. Sustainable AI therefore tracks both one-time training runs and recurring serving workloads rather than treating model training as the whole footprint.
  2. Distinction: Unlike corporate sustainability reporting (which aggregates energy usage into annual CO2 disclosures), sustainable AI engineering operates at the individual workload level. It selects hardware by FLOP/s per watt, schedules training during periods of high renewable availability, and chooses model architectures that minimize inference FLOPs rather than maximizing accuracy alone.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that switching to renewable energy solves the sustainability problem. For hardware-intensive ML, embodied carbon (the carbon emitted manufacturing the chips, servers, and cooling equipment before they ever run a training job) can rival operational carbon on clean grids or at low utilization. For battery-powered consumer devices, manufacturing can account for most of the lifecycle footprint (Gupta et al. 2022). Hardware longevity and utilization rate are therefore as important as energy source.
Li, Chengwei. 2020. Estimating the Training Cost of GPT-3.
Lannelongue, Loı̈c, Jason Grealey, and Michael Inouye. 2021. “Green Algorithms: Quantifying the Carbon Footprint of Computation.” Advanced Science 8 (12): 2100707. https://doi.org/10.1002/advs.202100707.
Wynsberghe, Aimee van. 2021. “Sustainable AI: AI for Sustainability and the Sustainability of AI.” AI and Ethics 1: 213–18. https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-021-00043-6.

The environmental impact of AI systems spans the complete lifecycle: from semiconductor manufacturing and data center construction to model training, inference deployment, and electronic waste (Wynsberghe 2021; Gupta et al. 2022; Luccioni et al. 2023). Treating this full lifecycle as an engineering problem rather than a corporate responsibility exercise transforms sustainability from a vague objective into a measurable engineering requirement. Optimizing this footprint requires first grounding intuition in the raw physical energy of a single large-model training run.

Any such calculation first has to fix what the energy measurement includes. This is the accounting discipline that the energy accounting boundary (equation 1.5) imposes on fleet comparisons, applied here to a single run. Accelerator energy excludes host processors, host memory, networking, and storage. IT energy includes those supporting systems; facility energy additionally includes cooling and facility-side power losses. Power usage effectiveness (PUE) is the ratio of facility energy to IT energy over the same interval (equation 2); section 15.4.1.3 gives its formal definition and what it leaves out. Dividing useful training FLOPs by the run’s fleet energy productivity, its useful FLOPs per joule, gives energy at the boundary over which that productivity was measured. If that boundary is IT equipment, multiplying by PUE gives facility energy; if it already includes the facility, applying PUE again double counts overhead. The opening calculations use one completed training run as their unit of work and account for operational electricity. Manufacturing and other lifecycle terms enter separately.

Checkpoint 15.1: The energy of intelligence

A 175B parameter model requires approximately \(3.14 \times 10^{23}\) FLOPs to train. Assuming a data center PUE of 1.1 and a realized training efficiency of 50 GFLOP/J measured across the IT equipment, including support systems but excluding facility overhead:

That household-year figure is the stake; it is also only the electricity entering one building for one run. Pricing its environmental impact requires converting those megawatt-hours into carbon emissions across regional power grids, placing single-run electricity draw inside global data-center demand, and accounting for the water, manufacturing, and hardware disposal burdens that accompany the electricity bill.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A hyperscaler plans a 500 MW campus for a new foundation model cluster, but the local utility interconnect approval is capped at 320 MW for the next three years. The organization’s capital reserves could cover the electricity cost multiple times over. Which framing best captures why sustainable AI treats energy as a first-class engineering constraint rather than a financial or reporting concern?

    1. Carbon accounting frameworks mandate disclosing total planned capacity upfront, creating an administrative filing hurdle before training begins.
    2. Electricity price fluctuations create budget volatility, so the primary engineering intervention is financial hedging.
    3. Corporate sustainability reporting requires purchasing voluntary offsets for unapproved megawatts, adding financial cost without altering physical infrastructure.
    4. Power delivery and grid interconnect capacity impose a hard physical ceiling that capital cannot bypass on deployment timescales, making power an operational constraint that model architectures and cluster sizing must fit within.

  2. A research team plans a 10,000 MWh training run, measured as facility electricity. Quebec and Poland both meet its capacity, data-access, quality, and deadline requirements, and transfer overhead is negligible. The stated grid factors are approximately 20 and 820 grams of CO2 per kWh, respectively, on a common boundary. An algorithmic change would reduce facility energy in Poland by 15 percent at unchanged quality. Calculate the emissions under these alternatives and justify which offers the larger reduction in this scenario.

  3. True or False: Because successive generations of specialized AI accelerators deliver substantial improvements in energy efficiency (FLOP/s per watt), organizations can rely on hardware innovation alone to keep total data center power consumption flat while scaling model parameters.

  4. Thousands of GPUs alternate between dense computation and collective communication, producing correlated changes in device power. Explain how bidirectional energy storage can buffer the change at a protected electrical boundary, and what measurements determine its timing and capacity.

See Answers →

15.2 The Scale of Environmental Impact

A training run measured in megawatt-hours is hard to reason about, because few engineers carry an intuition for what a megawatt-hour of grid electricity costs the atmosphere. The introduction quoted GPT-3’s training emissions as one of the costs that scaling imposes (Sustainability and cost implications). The derivation behind that figure converts the run’s energy into emissions with a grid factor and then into a familiar anchor, the CO2 of a trans-Atlantic flight, which turns an abstract number into one with physical stakes.

Napkin Math 15.1: The carbon cost of training
Problem: A team trains a large model (GPT-3 size), treating 1,287 MWh as facility electricity for this comparison. How much CO2 is emitted under the stated grid factor, and how does that compare to a trans-Atlantic flight?

Math:

  1. Energy: Training energy consumption is 1,287 MWh = 1,287,000 kWh.
  2. Carbon intensity (US average): \(\approx\) 0.429 kg/kWh.
  3. Total Emissions: Training emissions are 1,287,000 kWh \(\times\) 0.429 kg/kWh \(\approx\) 552,123 kg.
  4. Comparison:
    • One passenger, NY to London (round trip): \(\approx\) 1000 kg.
    • Ratio: 552,123 kg / 1000 kg = 552.1.

Systems insight: Under these assumptions, a single training run emits as much carbon as hundreds of trans-Atlantic passenger round trips. The calculation holds facility energy fixed, so changing the grid factor changes the attributed emissions. The same energy on a hydro-heavy grid would emit a small fraction as much, the placement lever that notebook 15.2 quantifies.

The same run’s emissions depend on where its electricity comes from. For a fixed workload, the grid emissions factor and the facility overhead together determine the attributed carbon, and the placement calculations separate those effects, starting with the grid factor alone.

The introduction previewed this geographic factor as a spread of roughly 41× between hydro- and coal-dominated grids. Notebook 15.2 derives that spread by holding facility energy constant and varying only the grid factor.

The same job’s carbon emissions depend on where it runs.

Napkin Math 15.2: The geography of carbon
Problem: A team is choosing a data center for a 10,000 MWh training run, comparing Site A in Quebec (hydropower, 20 g/kWh \(\text{CO}_2\)) against Site B in Poland (coal-heavy, 820 g/kWh \(\text{CO}_2\)). Both factors are rounded annual grid averages on the same emissions boundary, the values figure 6 later applies across nine regions; they are not hourly or marginal signals. How does the location affect a model’s carbon footprint?

Math: Carbon = Energy \(\times\) Grid Intensity.

  1. Site A emissions: 10,000,000 kWh \(\times\) 20 g/kWh = 200,000,000 g = 200 t \(\text{CO}_2\).
  2. Site B emissions: 10,000,000 kWh \(\times\) 820 g/kWh = 8,200,000,000 g = 8200 t \(\text{CO}_2\).
  3. Ratio: 8200 t / 200 t = 41× difference.

Systems insight: The Quebec-versus-Poland pair gives a 41× difference in attributed emissions at the same facility energy. This comparison isolates the grid factor; it does not rank placement against an unmeasured algorithmic change. A fleet decision must compare feasible sites and schedules using both workload energy and the emissions associated with supplying it.

The ratio holds only at equal facility electricity and these annual-average factors. A second gain from waiting for cleaner hours, the temporal lever of section 15.9.4, therefore cannot simply be multiplied onto it; the joint schedule must be evaluated as one decision.

The GPT-3 household-year calculation above anchors the workload scale. The fleet-scale consequence is larger. IEA projects global data-center electricity consumption to reach about 945 TWh by 2030, just under 3 percent of global electricity demand, with AI-accelerated servers driving much of the growth (International Energy Agency 2025).2 Historical studies document rapid growth in training compute (Sevilla et al. 2022), but a compute multiplier is not an energy multiplier without a matching efficiency measurement.

2 Data center industrial scale: The IEA projection is an electricity-demand metric, not directly comparable to aviation or cement shares of global emissions. AI infrastructure competes with other loads for deliverable power; expansion requires available capacity, additional supply, or a reduction or shift in competing demand, not necessarily new generation within the same region.

3 GPU manufacturing embodied carbon: NVIDIA’s HGX H100 product carbon footprint reports 1,312 kg CO2e (CO2-equivalent, which expresses other greenhouse gases as the mass of CO2 with the same warming effect) for an eight-H100 baseboard, or roughly 164 kg CO2e per H100 if allocated evenly across the eight GPUs (NVIDIA Corporation 2025). Advanced-node manufacturing also consumes ultrapure water, specialty gases, and chemicals that an energy-only account never sees, so a per-GPU carbon figure understates the full manufacturing footprint.

4 AI hardware e-waste: Retiring an accelerator does not necessarily create waste: equipment may be redeployed, resold, or recycled. Estimate disposal impacts from the actual equipment inventory, material composition, and end-of-life route. Extending useful service life can defer manufacturing and disposal, while certified handling limits exposure to hazardous materials in discarded electronics.

Beyond direct energy consumption, AI systems drive environmental impact through hardware manufacturing and resource consumption. Producing processors and their supporting equipment requires materials and industrial processes whose impacts precede the first training run.3 At the other end of the hardware’s life, retired equipment joins the electronic-waste stream whose scale section 15.8.5 quantifies. Rising workload requirements can motivate accelerator replacement before physical failure; whether replacement creates waste depends on the equipment’s reuse and disposal path.4

These environmental challenges are part of the energy ceiling, not an add-on to it, because the communities that host land, water, grid capacity, and waste streams also carry part of the system cost. These scale calculations turn sustainability into an allocation problem. AI progress creates benefits in one part of the system while assigning electricity, water, land, and disposal costs to another, so environmental responsibility must be treated as part of systems design rather than as postdeployment reporting (section 15.10.4 examines how site selection distributes these burdens across communities). A static snapshot of today’s footprint still understates the engineering challenge, however, because the computational demand driving these electricity, water, and hardware budgets is compounding far faster than hardware efficiency can absorb it.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A 2,000 MWh training run can be placed at a site whose annual-average grid factor is 400 g CO2 per kWh or at one whose factor is 20 g CO2 per kWh, with facility electricity held equal. Which conclusion follows?

    1. The cleaner site also cuts the run’s energy twentyfold, because low-carbon grids deliver electricity more efficiently.
    2. Attributed emissions fall from about 800 tonnes to about 40 tonnes, a twentyfold difference that isolates the grid factor at equal facility energy.
    3. This comparison alone ranks placement against an algorithmic change, because both reduce emissions.
    4. Waiting for cleaner hours at the cleaner site would multiply the saving by a further twentyfold.

  2. True or False: Because training compute for notable models has grown by many orders of magnitude, training energy has grown by the same multiplier.

  3. An operator reports its AI fleet’s environmental footprint as metered electricity multiplied by the grid factor. What does this account leave out?

    1. Nothing, because operational electricity is the only environmental cost of AI hardware.
    2. Only facility overhead, because grid factors already include the cost of manufacturing.
    3. A fixed disposal charge per device, because retiring any accelerator necessarily creates e-waste.
    4. Manufacturing impacts that precede the first training run, such as embodied carbon, ultrapure water, and specialty gases, and the land, water, and grid capacity that host communities supply.

  4. A Quebec-versus-Poland comparison shows a 41-fold emissions difference for the same 10,000 MWh run, and carbon-aware scheduling can also shift work to cleaner hours. Explain why the geographic and temporal gains cannot simply be multiplied, and how the decision should be evaluated.

See Answers →

15.3 Exponential Growth vs. Physical Constraints

Between 2012 and 2023, the training compute of frontier models expanded from roughly \(4.7 \times 10^{17}\) FLOPs for AlexNet to \(2 \times 10^{25}\) for GPT-4-class systems, an increase of about 45 million in 11 years that outpaced hardware efficiency gains by orders of magnitude. Sevilla et al. (2022) estimate that the training compute of notable machine learning models doubled roughly every six months after 2010, far faster than the two-year cadence historically associated with transistor scaling. The introduction traced this growth from AlexNet onward across seven to eight orders of magnitude (Where Training-Compute Growth Comes From), and figure 1 extends the same series through Grok-3 in 2025, carrying the span to nearly nine orders of magnitude against a fitted doubling cadence of roughly 5 months. Larger models, longer training runs, and rising serving volume each add to that total work through a different mechanism. Energy per operation can fall while total fleet energy rises; the outcome depends on how much useful work the fleet performs and how efficiently it performs that work.

Historically, semiconductor scaling combined greater transistor density with lower switching energy. Moore’s Law described the growth in transistor count, while Dennard scaling explained how reducing dimensions and voltage together could preserve power density. Transistor count alone did not guarantee better energy efficiency. Once voltage scaling encountered leakage and other device limits, a denser chip could no longer assume proportional power savings.5 Further efficiency gains require improvements in devices, architecture, and the work performed by the application.

5 Dennard scaling: Robert Dennard observed in 1974 that smaller transistors could operate at constant power density by reducing voltage proportionally. This scaling pattern ended around 2005 when leakage current made further voltage reduction impractical. The discussion of Dennard scaling in Power and thermal envelopes drew the facility consequence, that every refresh must fit installed power and cooling. The consequence for AI sustainability is equally direct: without Dennard scaling, each process node no longer delivers proportional power savings, which pushes efficiency work toward specialized accelerators, such as GPUs and the Tensor Processing Unit (TPU), that exploit architectural parallelism rather than transistor physics alone.

At a fixed accounting boundary, energy depends on total work divided by realized work per joule. Improvements in execution efficiency reduce IT energy, and PUE then adds the facility overhead on top of that IT energy. Algorithmic changes can also reduce the work required to meet a quality target. These effects must be measured together rather than inferred from a historical compute curve.

Per-job efficiency and total demand can move in opposite directions. Fleet energy is the energy per completed job multiplied by the number of completed jobs, so a fleet forecast must state both factors, including the workload mix. Cheaper jobs can raise the job count faster than they lower the energy of each one. If energy per task falls tenfold while task volume rises a hundredfold, fleet energy rises tenfold. This rebound is a second way demand can outrun efficiency, distinct from the scaling-driven growth described at the start of the chapter. Scaling-driven growth concerns how much compute each frontier model requires as scaling laws push quality upward; rebound concerns how many tasks run once each task becomes cheaper. The mitigation strategies of section 15.9 treat rebound under its historical name, the Jevons paradox.

15.3.1 Power envelopes and the energy wall

A short training burst can trip substation breakers or overwhelm chillers even when its cumulative megawatt-hour budget is modest, whereas a month-long job on a smaller cluster may consume far more total energy while staying safely inside its power envelope. The energy account divides work by realized work per joule and applies facility overhead at the boundary. Physical infrastructure, however, imposes a separate instantaneous power constraint: the grid connection, electrical distribution, and cooling system limit how much active fleet can operate safely at once.

The two limits obey different arithmetic (figure 2). The power limit is instantaneous. A facility cannot draw more than its grid feed (\(\FacilityPower\le \GridFeedCapacity\)), which caps the number of servers active at once (\(\PoweredServerCount\le \InformationTechnologyPower/ \ServerPower\)) regardless of how long a job runs. The energy account is cumulative, integrating average power over wall-clock time to give run energy (\(\TotalEnergy = \AveragePower\times \JobDuration\)). Exceeding electrical or thermal capacity can force power limiting, throttling, or protective shutdown. Cumulative energy instead enters the electricity and emissions accounts, which also depend on tariffs and the applicable grid emissions factors.

The energy wall is therefore a workload-specific feasibility problem, not a universal multiplier. Engineers must fit the required work, deadline, and quality target within available power and cooling, then calculate energy over the actual runtime. More efficient hardware helps only to the extent that the workload uses its efficiency and the resulting demand remains within those constraints. At frontier scale, the binding question is whether enough power can be delivered to the cluster at all.

Lighthouse 15.1: Archetype A (GPT-4/Llama-3): The energy wall
Training at the scale of Archetype A (GPT-4/Llama-3) exposes the power problem most sharply. At frontier scale, a single 25,000-GPU cluster is roughly 24 times the 1,024-GPU reference cluster used throughout this book. At 700 W per chip, it requires 17.5 MW of accelerator power before server, network, and cooling overhead. The constraint is physical, not financial: it is a grid capacity problem. Organizations operating Archetype A workloads may need dedicated power infrastructure or regions with excess renewable energy. Carbon-aware scheduling (shifting nonurgent work toward cleaner grid hours) and geographic optimization (placing workloads where power, cooling, and carbon constraints fit the job) therefore become fleet decisions as consequential as capacity planning.

That accelerator draw alone, before server, network, and cooling overhead, makes grid capacity a condition of deployment. Even when the grid can supply that sustained average power, the synchronized execution of a training job swings the load up and down across the cluster in unison.

15.3.2 Data center grid dynamics

When thousands of accelerators enter a collective communication barrier simultaneously and then resume matrix multiplication together, their power draw steps down and up in lockstep across the data hall. Unlike conventional cloud workloads whose independent requests smooth out across racks, synchronized training phases correlate power swings and push those transients toward the electrical grid interface.

War Story 15.1: When the grid became the bottleneck (2022)
Context: In July 2022, the Government of Ireland published its Government Statement on the Role of Data Centres in Ireland’s Enterprise Strategy, addressing electricity constraints on further development in a country where data centers had become a substantial source of demand (Government of Ireland 2022; International Energy Agency 2024).

Mechanism: Demand growth outpaced grid expansion. The statement reported that Dublin needed significant transmission reinforcement before it could accommodate new data-center power requests.

Impact: Grid connection capacity became the gating input for proposed Dublin facilities. New requests had to wait for transmission reinforcement or look to other regions, while contracted projects and existing sites continued to operate.

Fix: The statement prioritized network reinforcement, suitable regional locations, and development aligned with additional renewable supply. These were proposed responses to the constraint, not evidence that operators had already resolved it.

Systems lesson: Sustainable AI is constrained by interconnection, geography, and power-system adequacy. Efficient accelerators help, but the fleet cannot scale faster than the grid that feeds it, moving the binding constraint from the chip to the substation.

International Energy Agency. 2024. Data Centre Electricity Demand Estimates as a Share of Total Electricity Demand in Ireland and Virginia, 2023. IEA chart.
Government of Ireland. 2022. Government Statement on the Role of Data Centres in Ireland’s Enterprise Strategy. Government policy statement.

Training phases can change accelerator demand rapidly, but a device-side load step does not propagate unchanged to the utility connection. Its amplitude depends on per-device power, the number of coordinated workers, and computation-communication overlap. Local regulators, decoupling, power converters, and UPS controls shape the upstream response. Energy buffering supplies or absorbs energy during a load change; its location, capacity, and control response must match the electrical boundary being protected.

15.3.3 Training-scale energy concentration

The grid and siting constraints become severe because large training campaigns concentrate energy demand into long, synchronized runs. The GPT-3 anchor established the total scale; the new systems concern is its temporal concentration and the additional energy from distributed-training communication.6

6 Training communication overhead: Distributed training adds network transfers, synchronization stalls, and checkpoint I/O to the energy account. The fraction is workload- and topology-dependent: parallelism strategy, tensor sizes, link technology, utilization loss, and any extra iterations determine whether communication is a secondary term or the binding energy cost. The measurement contract must therefore meter or model these terms for the actual run rather than apply a universal percentage.

Concentration in time is what separates a training campaign from the same energy spread across a year of ordinary load. A frontier run holds the 17.5 MW accelerator draw of the Archetype A cluster, before servers, network, and cooling, for the weeks or months the run lasts. Its synchronized computation and communication phases swing that draw together across the cluster (section 15.3.2). The facility must therefore provision for the sustained peak rather than for the annual average, and the grid sees one large, correlated load rather than many small independent ones. That concentration makes efficiency improvements an engineering imperative. Large generative models intensify the problem when successive generations increase parameter counts, token budgets, or both.

The scaling laws introduced in AI Scaling Laws explain why that concentration keeps growing. Within the ranges studied by Kaplan et al. (2020), test loss falls along smooth power laws in compute, dataset tokens, and parameters (figure 3), and for an energy account the small compute exponent is the quantity of interest. It means each fixed fractional reduction in loss costs a multiplicative increase in the resource. Because required compute scales as the loss ratio raised to the power 20, lowering compute-limited loss by 10 percent takes 8.2× the compute, and the next 10 percent costs the same multiple again. The diminishing return in principle 9 therefore arrives at the facility as a multiplicative increase in training energy unless realized FLOPs per joule improve at the same pace. Within these studied regimes, quality gains were purchasable with hardware and data, which is why compute demand has outrun hardware efficiency. Beyond training, high-volume deployed systems such as large-scale recommender systems and generative services require continuous inference at scale, consuming energy even after training completes. The cumulative energy burden therefore depends on both the one-time training run and the sustained query volume that follows deployment.

Building out the clusters required by this concentration adds emissions before an operator runs a single training step, so expanding the fleet increases manufacturing impact even when each new accelerator uses less energy per useful task. An operator’s inventory records that fabrication under Scope 3, the category for supply-chain emissions rather than on-site generation or purchased electricity (section 15.5.5 quantifies the manufacturing term, and section 15.5.2 assigns it to reporting scopes). Bounding any of these operational or embodied costs requires decomposing facility power into component-level models and direct telemetry under a consistent measurement contract.

Self-Check: Question

  1. An inference optimization cuts energy per task by a factor of 4, and the lower cost raises task volume by a factor of 20, with the workload mix unchanged. What happens to fleet energy?

    1. It falls by a factor of 4, because per-task efficiency sets fleet energy.
    2. It stays the same, because efficiency and demand cancel.
    3. It rises by a factor of 5, because fleet energy is energy per task multiplied by the number of tasks.
    4. It rises by a factor of 80, because the efficiency gain and the demand growth compound.

  2. A team plans a three-day run that needs 120 MW of accelerator power at once in a facility with an 80 MW grid feed. They argue the run fits because its total energy is less than the facility uses in a month. What is wrong with the argument?

    1. The power limit is instantaneous, so the feed caps how many servers can be active at once regardless of run length; the plan must lower concurrency and accept a longer run, or obtain more deliverable power.
    2. Nothing, because over a month an energy budget and a power cap are interchangeable.
    3. The run fails only on cost, because energy drawn above the feed is billed at a peak tariff.
    4. The only error is omitting PUE; adding facility overhead would show that the run’s energy exceeds the monthly budget.

  3. Synchronized training phases swing accelerator draw together across thousands of devices, and a battery or UPS stage that supplies or absorbs energy during each load change provides energy ____, whose location, capacity, and control response must match the electrical boundary being protected.

  4. True or False: Because a frontier training run’s yearly energy is a modest share of a region’s consumption, the facility can be provisioned for that run’s annual-average power.

  5. In the regime studied by Kaplan et al., each 10 percent reduction in compute-limited loss costs about 8.2 times the compute. Explain what two successive 10 percent reductions imply for training energy, and what would have to hold for energy not to grow by the same factor.

See Answers →

15.4 Energy Measurement and Modeling

A cluster drawing five megawatts during a large language model training run directs only a fraction of that power into matrix multiplications; host processors, memory, networking, power conversion, and cooling consume the rest. Engineers cannot optimize those losses from a single monthly utility bill: effective energy modeling requires decomposing facility power into granular, component-level metrics that engineers can target for optimization.

Power delivery networks and cooling systems establish hard physical constraints on data center compute capacity. Systematic measurement turns these constraints into numbers an engineer can act on, provided the measurement fixes what it records and which lifecycle phase it targets before any power model or instrument is applied. Just as performance engineering requires profiling before optimization, sustainable AI engineering requires measurement before mitigation.

↩︎ The power-delivery path, thermal design power, and cooling limits of accelerators are established in Power and thermal envelopes.

The decision procedure is the same throughout the chapter: measure the dominant lifecycle term, identify the physical bottleneck behind it, choose the intervention that changes that term, and check whether rebound effects erase the gain. Operational electricity, embodied carbon, cooling overhead, water use, and e-waste each call for different levers. Moving a job changes its operational setting without undoing emissions already incurred in manufacturing. Keeping hardware longer changes future procurement needs but also extends its operating energy account. Sustainable design therefore traces each intervention through the terms it changes before comparing the full result.

A sustainability claim is comparable only when it satisfies a sustainability measurement contract, which extends the reproducible measurement contract of The measurement contract. That contract already fixes the workload and its quality gate, the system state, the timing boundary, and a statistical report with its window and spread. A sustainability claim adds five fields that progressively close the remaining accounting degrees of freedom. The functional unit names the work being charged, such as one completed training run or 1,000 served requests at a specified output quality. The system boundary states which compute, memory, network, facility, manufacturing, and end-of-life terms enter that account (International Organization for Standardization 2006a, 2006b). Because multiple workloads and hardware generations share a facility, an attribution rule must then allocate shared infrastructure and embodied emissions. A declared average or marginal carbon signal, recorded with its time and location, converts the resulting energy into emissions. Finally, the statistical report extends to the sensitivity of the conclusion to those consequential assumptions. A result that omits any of these fields may still diagnose one component, but it cannot support an end-to-end comparison.

Present, Elaina, Pieter Gagnon, Eric J. H. Wilson, Noel Merket, Philip R. White, and Scott Horowitz. 2024. Choosing the Best Carbon Factor for the Job: Exploring Available Carbon Emissions Factors and the Impact of Factor Selection. NREL/CP-5500-86682. National Renewable Energy Laboratory.

This contract separates two questions that otherwise become conflated. Attributional accounting allocates an inventory of observed emissions to workloads for reporting, typically using average grid intensity and an explicit share of common infrastructure. Consequential accounting estimates how a decision changes emissions, for example by modeling the generator affected when a scheduler moves one megawatt-hour of demand. The former answers what share of an existing footprint belongs to a workload; the latter answers whether changing the workload changes the footprint (Present et al. 2024). Carbon-aware scheduling therefore needs a signal appropriate for consequential analysis even when the organization’s retrospective inventory uses attributional averages. The available marginal factors may be historical estimates, forecasts, or unavailable at the workload’s required time and location. The contract must therefore record the source, resolution, and uncertainty rather than treat “marginal” as a universally observable datum.

15.4.1 Power modeling fundamentals

Lowering numerical precision or pruning half the weights of a model does not automatically cut the data center’s electricity bill in half, because static leakage, memory traffic, and facility cooling each take their own share before power reaches the arithmetic units. Tracing where a watt goes requires a model that climbs three levels. The CMOS power equation at the chip level explains why voltage, precision, and switching activity matter. Physical optimization mechanisms show how algorithms change those variables. Facility-level metrics reveal whether chip-level savings survive cooling and power-delivery overhead.

The CMOS power equation

Every digital circuit consumes power through two fundamental mechanisms. Dynamic power arises from switching transistors between states, while static power results from leakage current that flows even when transistors are nominally off. Equation 1 formalizes the total power consumption:

\[\TotalChipPower= \DynamicPower+ \LeakagePower = \SwitchingActivityFactor \SwitchedCapacitance \SupplyVoltage^2 \ClockFrequency+ \SupplyVoltage \LeakageCurrent \tag{1}\]

In the dynamic component \(\DynamicPower= \SwitchingActivityFactor \SwitchedCapacitance \SupplyVoltage^2 \ClockFrequency\), the switching activity factor \(\SwitchingActivityFactor\) summarizes capacitance-weighted switching per clock cycle and is not useful compute utilization. The term \(\SwitchedCapacitance\) is the effective load capacitance of the circuits and interconnect being switched. Supply voltage \(\SupplyVoltage\) enters quadratically, and clock frequency \(\ClockFrequency\) sets the rate of switching opportunities. Useful work per joule depends on how much productive computation those transitions accomplish.

The static power component \(\LeakagePower = \SupplyVoltage\cdot \LeakageCurrent\) depends on leakage current, whose temperature sensitivity varies with device technology, voltage, and operating point. Rising temperature can increase leakage, adding heat that further raises temperature. Cooling and power control must keep this feedback within a stable operating range. A leakage model therefore needs parameters appropriate to the device rather than a universal temperature-doubling rule (Dayarathna et al. 2016).

Dayarathna, Miyuru, Yonggang Wen, and Rui Fan. 2016. “Data Center Energy Consumption Modeling: A Survey.” IEEE Communications Surveys & Tutorials 18 (1): 732–94. https://doi.org/10.1109/comst.2015.2481183.

The quadratic voltage dependence means that reducing voltage from 1 V to 0.8 V decreases dynamic power by 36 percent when activity, capacitance, and frequency stay fixed. The circuit must still complete its transitions within each clock period. A lower voltage may therefore require a lower frequency, which further reduces dynamic power but can lengthen execution. The energy benefit depends on both the resulting runtime and the static power consumed during it.

Physical mechanisms of energy optimization

The power equation illuminates why specific optimization techniques can improve efficiency. Quantization reduces numerical precision, which can shrink arithmetic units and reduce bytes moved through the memory hierarchy. The realized energy change depends on the implemented kernels, packing and conversion overhead, memory traffic, utilization, and the original bottleneck (Gholami et al. 2022). Lower precision does not by itself permit a lower supply voltage: voltage and frequency remain constrained by the physical implementation, process, and timing margins. Quantization therefore changes the operation and data-movement terms, but only measurement establishes the end-to-end reduction.

Gholami, Amir, Sehoon Kim, Zhen Dong, Zhewei Yao, Michael W. Mahoney, and Kurt Keutzer. 2022. “A Survey of Quantization Methods for Efficient Neural Network Inference.” In Low-Power Computer Vision: Improve the Efficiency of Artificial Intelligence. Chapman; Hall/CRC. https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003162810-13.

Pruning removes weights from neural networks, but it does not remove physical transistors from an existing accelerator. It saves dynamic energy when the resulting execution skips arithmetic or data movement. Structured pruning can map to smaller dense kernels; unstructured pruning needs an implementation that exploits the sparse pattern without spending the savings on indexing and irregular memory access. The power equation explains the benefit through reduced switching activity and powered execution time, not a change to the fabricated circuit capacitance \(\SwitchedCapacitance\).

Specialized accelerators can improve useful work per joule by reducing instruction-control overhead, reusing data locally, and mapping operations to suitable datapaths. Keeping arithmetic units productive can amortize static and support power, but raising switching activity without raising useful output only raises power.

Facility-level power metrics

The PUE ratio has carried every facility-energy estimate since the opening energy boundary. The chip-level model now explains what happens inside the IT term, so the ratio itself needs its precise definition and its blind spots. Equation 2 gives the ratio at this chapter’s facility boundary:

\[\text{PUE} = \frac{\FacilityPower}{\InformationTechnologyPower} \tag{2}\]

Definition 15.2: Power usage effectiveness (PUE)

Power usage effectiveness (PUE) is the data-center efficiency ratio ML system operators use to compare total facility power consumption against the power consumed specifically by IT equipment \((\FacilityPower/ \InformationTechnologyPower)\).

  1. Significance: It measures the infrastructure overhead of the data center. A PUE of 1.0 is the theoretical ideal; a PUE of 1.10 means that for every 100 watts consumed by IT equipment, an additional 10 watts are required for non-IT facility overhead, including cooling and facility-side power distribution.
  2. Distinction: Unlike computing efficiency (which focuses on FLOP/s per watt), PUE focuses on facility efficiency: it measures non-IT overhead relative to IT consumption. Idle equipment and power losses inside the IT measurement boundary remain in the denominator; PUE does not measure useful computation.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that a low PUE means a “green” data center. PUE measures only overhead efficiency, not the carbon intensity of the energy source; a coal-powered data center can have a better PUE than a solar-powered one while having a much higher environmental impact.

PUE measures facility overhead on top of IT energy.

Napkin Math 15.3: PUE: The cost of cooling

Problem: A team operates a 2 MW cluster. If the facility can be optimized from a legacy air-cooled PUE (1.58) to state-of-the-art (1.10), how much energy and money does that save annually?

Math: Energy saved is the difference in infrastructure overhead \((\text{PUE}-1)\) across the IT load.

  1. Overhead Reduction: 1.58 - 1.10 = 0.48.
  2. Annual energy savings: 2 MW \(\times\) 0.48 \(\times\) 8760 h/year \(\approx\) 8,409.6 MWh.
  3. Financial Savings: 8,409.6 MWh \(\times\) $70/MWh \(\approx\) $588,672. This facility-only saving leaves the model unchanged and scales with reducible non-IT overhead, so cooling optimization matters most in inefficient facilities.

A PUE of 1.0 would mean no measured non-IT overhead, not that all electricity produces useful computation. Idle servers and stalled accelerators remain inside the IT denominator. Surveyed data centers reported an average PUE near 1.55 in 2022, meaning that roughly 55 percent additional energy beyond IT consumption went to infrastructure, with older air-cooled sites higher still (Uptime Institute 2022). Leading hyperscale facilities achieve PUE between 1.1 and 1.2 through advanced cooling techniques including free-air cooling in cold climates, liquid cooling for high-density GPU clusters, and optimized power distribution.

Uptime Institute. 2022. Uptime Institute Global Data Center Survey 2022. Uptime Institute.

Equation 3 formalizes water usage effectiveness (WUE), capturing the water consumption that evaporative cooling and other processes require:

\[\text{WUE} = \frac{\FacilityWaterConsumption}{\InformationTechnologyEnergy} \tag{3}\]

The units are liters per kilowatt-hour, with typical values ranging from 0.5 to 2.0 L/kWh depending on climate and cooling technology. A data center with WUE of 1.8 L/kWh training a model requiring 10,000 MWh of IT energy would consume 18 million liters of water. That is equivalent to roughly 40–47 US household-years of water use under a 380,000–450,000 L/year household baseline. A facility-energy estimate must first be converted to the IT boundary before applying this WUE.

Serving has a water account too, and it is computed the same way. Google reports a fleet WUE of 1.15 L/kWh for the data centers that serve its Gemini assistant (Elsworth et al. 2025). Applied to the IT energy of the median prompt measured in section 15.7.3, which excludes facility overhead, that WUE gives the 0.26 mL of water per prompt that the study reports. A single prompt’s water is negligible. A service answering billions of prompts a day turns the same arithmetic into a facility-scale demand, which is why the cooling choice, and the watershed it draws on, belongs in the serving design rather than only in the training budget. Facility-level metrics therefore identify where engineering intervention yields the greatest returns, including in how a facility operates the cooling plant it already has.

DeepMind energy efficiency case study

Google’s cooling systems illustrate how operating settings affect PUE (2023). Server load, weather, and interactions among pumps and chillers complicate fixed operating heuristics (Buyya et al. 2010). Building on earlier work predicting PUE (Gao 2014), DeepMind trained neural networks from facility telemetry to predict future PUE and recommend settings. Additional models predicted temperature and pressure to check those recommendations against operating constraints.

Google. 2023. Power Usage Effectiveness: Google Data Centers.
Buyya, Rajkumar, Anton Beloglazov, and Jemal Abawajy. 2010. “Energy-Efficient Management of Data Center Resources for Cloud Computing: A Vision, Architectural Elements, and Open Challenges.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1006.0308, 6–17. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.1006.0308.
Gao, J. 2014. Machine Learning Applications for Data Center Optimization. Google; Google White Paper.
Barroso, Luiz André, Urs Hölzle, and Parthasarathy Ranganathan. 2019. The Datacenter as a Computer: Designing Warehouse-Scale Machines. Synthesis Lectures on Computer Architecture. Springer International Publishing. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-01761-2.
Evans, Richard, and Jim Gao. 2016. DeepMind AI Reduces Google Data Centre Cooling Bill by 40%. DeepMind Blog.

In the reported live-site test, the system reduced cooling energy consumption by 40 percent and overall PUE overhead by 15 percent (Barroso et al. 2019; Evans and Gao 2016). By equation 2, a 15 percent cut in overhead removes 15 percent of the non-IT term (PUE minus 1), not 15 percent of total facility energy. At equal IT energy, the same fractional overhead reduction saves more electricity where the initial overhead is larger. The reported result establishes a benefit for the tested facility, not an identical saving at every site.

The saving came from changing how the existing pumps, chillers, and cooling towers were operated rather than from installing new cooling equipment, which makes control software a lever available to a facility that cannot be rebuilt. The transferable lesson is to evaluate operating controls alongside equipment changes. Telemetry supports the prediction, operating limits constrain the recommendation, and metered energy establishes whether the change saves electricity.

15.4.2 Systematic energy metrics

Cutting a model’s FLOP count in half saves almost no energy when the workload spends nearly all of its joules shuttling weights between off-chip DRAM and the processor. Comparing hardware architectures and algorithmic changes therefore requires a systematic metric ladder that moves from energy per operation to energy per byte and then to the roofline relationship between arithmetic intensity and data movement.

Energy per operation

A basic metric for computational energy efficiency is energy consumed per operation, typically measured in picojoules. State the operation type and precision: a floating-point multiply-accumulate (MAC) is conventionally two FLOPs, whereas an integer MAC is two integer arithmetic operations.

Hardware architecture changes which overheads can be reduced. Each architecture class carries an effective energy coefficient in this chapter’s models, paired with the measurement needed to test it (table 1). The coefficients span three orders of magnitude, from 100 pJ/FLOP for a general-purpose core, where instruction fetch and control dominate each operation, to 0.1 pJ/FLOP for a fixed-function datapath. They fold local operand delivery and control into each operation, which is why the GPU coefficient of 10 pJ/FLOP sits well above the roughly 3.7 pJ of a bare FP32 multiply at 45 nm (Horowitz 2014). They are order-of-magnitude modeling inputs, not a ranking of products.

Horowitz, Mark. 2014. “1.1 Computing’s Energy Problem (and What We Can Do about It).” 2014 IEEE International Solid-State Circuits Conference Digest of Technical Papers (ISSCC), 10–14. https://doi.org/10.1109/isscc.2014.6757323.

Precision affects energy per operation through implementation-specific arithmetic and data movement. Narrower formats can reduce circuit area, switched capacitance, and memory traffic, but bit width alone does not determine wall energy. The measurement contract requires a matched workload and quality target, with the same memory and supporting-system boundary.

Energy per byte

In AI workloads with low arithmetic intensity, moving operands across storage and interconnect boundaries consumes far more energy than the arithmetic units themselves. An illustrative cost model across registers, caches, main memory, solid-state storage, and the network makes this hierarchy concrete; its coefficients teach the relative cost of movement across boundaries rather than fixed measurements of every platform.

Fetching an FP32 operand from DRAM costs 64× the FLOP that uses it.

With the modeled coefficients of table 2, movement from DRAM can dominate the arithmetic it feeds. The rows trace the path from registers through L1 cache and L2 cache to DRAM, Non-Volatile Memory Express (NVMe), and network transfers. Under the GPU coefficient of 10 pJ/FLOP from table 1, accessing one FP32 operand from DRAM (4 bytes \(\times\) 160 pJ/byte = 640 pJ) costs 64× the energy of the operation that consumes it. These coefficients agree with the movement costs priced per bit in Energy cost of data movement once the units match. That section’s off-package DRAM figure of about 20 pJ per bit is this table’s 160 pJ/byte, so one 16-bit operand fetched from DRAM costs 320 pJ, where this example fetches a 4-byte FP32 operand. The bare FP16 multiply-accumulate of about 1.5 pJ quoted there sits below the effective GPU coefficient here because that coefficient folds control and local operand delivery into each operation. Transfers across the network fabric are priced per bit in The Physics of Data Movement, whose fabric-path figure of about 35 pJ/bit (280 pJ/byte) charges only the serializers, transceivers, and switches between endpoints. The network row here is a whole-system coefficient that also charges endpoint memory, host software, and idle network power, so the two differ by more than an order of magnitude.

The resulting design levers target data movement as well as arithmetic. On-chip SRAM and registers support operand reuse, while systolic arrays pass operands and partial sums between neighboring processing elements instead of repeatedly returning to off-chip memory. Compression and sparse representations can reduce transferred data when saved payload exceeds metadata and conversion overhead. The energy benefit therefore depends on the implemented memory traffic, rather than solely on nominal model size or arithmetic throughput.

Arithmetic intensity and energy roofline

↩︎ The performance roofline and its ridge point are established in Roofline model.

Arithmetic intensity, the operations-per-byte ratio recalled in Arithmetic Intensity Boundary, connects the operation count to the data-movement volume. Combined with energy per operation and per byte, it identifies which contribution dominates this energy model; it does not by itself identify the resource limiting execution speed. Equation 4 defines the workload ratio. In these equations, \(\Operations\) is the operation count in FLOPs, \(\DataVolume\) is data volume in bytes, \(\EnergyPerOperation\) is energy per FLOP, and \(\EnergyPerByteMoved\) is energy per byte moved:

\[\ArithmeticIntensity= \frac{\Operations}{\DataVolume} \tag{4}\]

At fixed energy coefficients, arithmetic intensity measured in FLOP/byte determines the relative size of the compute and movement terms. Equation 5 expresses modeled energy \(\TotalEnergy\) at the chip-and-memory boundary as their sum (the facility total \(\FacilityEnergy\) appears later in equation 9), while equation 6 isolates the roofline-style dominant term. Static power and supporting systems require additional terms if the comparison includes them:

\[\TotalEnergy = \Operations\times \EnergyPerOperation + \DataVolume \times \EnergyPerByteMoved \tag{5}\]

\[\DominantComponentEnergy= \max\left(\Operations\times \EnergyPerOperation, \DataVolume \times \EnergyPerByteMoved\right) \tag{6}\]

The maximum identifies the larger energy contribution, not the sum. Near balance, omitting the other term substantially understates the modeled energy. Equation 7 defines the crossover arithmetic intensity where compute and memory energy balance:

\[\EnergyCrossoverIntensity= \frac{\EnergyPerByteMoved}{\EnergyPerOperation} \tag{7}\]

For a GPU with \(\EnergyPerOperation\) of 10 pJ/FLOP and \(\EnergyPerByteMoved\) of 160 pJ/byte (DRAM access):

\[\EnergyCrossoverIntensity = \frac{160 \text{ pJ/byte}}{10 \text{ pJ/FLOP}} = 16 \text{ FLOP/byte}\]

The energy roofline model in figure 4 bounds energy efficiency (GFLOP/J) against arithmetic intensity (FLOP/byte), with a 16 FLOP/byte crossover under the stated component costs. Below this point, the larger energy contribution is memory movement (160 pJ/byte). Above it, arithmetic sets the horizontal upper bound of 100 GFLOP/J (10 pJ/FLOP). The piecewise bound keeps only the larger contribution, whereas actual energy includes both and any omitted system overhead. Its crossover therefore answers a different question from the performance ridge, which equals peak arithmetic throughput divided by memory bandwidth and often sits well above 16 FLOP/byte on modern accelerators. A kernel whose arithmetic intensity falls between the energy crossover and the performance ridge is still paced in execution time by memory bandwidth even though arithmetic already accounts for more of its modeled dynamic energy. Applying this crossover to matrix multiplication shows how problem dimension alone flips the dominant energy term.

Napkin Math 15.4: MatMul energy analysis
Problem: How do matrix dimensions \(\SquareGemmDimension\) dictate whether arithmetic energy \(\EnergyPerOperation\) or memory energy \(\EnergyPerByteMoved\) dominates matrix multiplication \(\GemmOutputMatrix= \GemmLeftOperand\GemmRightOperand\)?

Math:

  1. Operation & byte count: \(2\SquareGemmDimension^3\) FLOPs and \(12\SquareGemmDimension^2\) bytes at FP32, assuming ideal reuse: read each input once and write the output once at the modeled memory boundary. Tiling and limited capacity can increase measured traffic.
  2. Arithmetic intensity: \(\ArithmeticIntensity= \frac{\SquareGemmDimension}{6}\) FLOP/byte, so the matrix sits exactly at the energy crossover when \(\SquareGemmDimension\) = 96 (\(\ArithmeticIntensity = 96/6 = 16\)), the small-matrix row below.
  3. Regime comparison: small dense work sits at the crossover, large dense work is dominated by arithmetic, and element-wise work by memory movement (table 3).

Systems insight: Energy optimization follows the same bottleneck logic as latency optimization. Dense matrix multiply rewards efficient arithmetic; element-wise work rewards reducing memory movement.

The energy roofline model reveals why different optimization strategies suit different workloads. When arithmetic dominates the energy account, large dense matrix operations benefit from lower energy per executed operation. When data movement dominates, element-wise kernels benefit from data layout optimization, kernel fusion to reduce memory round-trips, and on-chip memory utilization. Faster execution alone does not establish either saving; average power and execution time must be measured together. This framework guides architectural and algorithmic choices for sustainable AI system design.

15.4.3 Energy measurement techniques

Reading nvidia-smi during a training step misses host DRAM, NVMe storage, network switches, and chiller pumps, whereas reading the substation utility meter lumps every co-located job into a single total. Quantifying AI system energy consumption therefore requires choosing measurement instruments across multiple levels of the hardware stack. On-chip counters provide fine attribution, mobile profilers expose platform-specific subsystems, external edge instruments capture duty-cycle behavior, and system-level tools connect component measurements to the facility overhead that device counters miss.

Hardware power counters

Modern processors expose manufacturer-provided power telemetry that software can query through device interfaces. These counters provide workload-correlated estimates within the rails and sampling windows the device reports; they are not universal ground truth for wall-plug energy, and their update rate, calibration, and component coverage vary by platform. External instruments remain necessary when a comparison requires traceable total energy or finer temporal resolution.

Intel’s running average power limit (RAPL) interface exposes cumulative energy counters for supported power domains, with coverage depending on the processor. A controlled measurement samples the relevant counter before and after the workload and divides the energy delta by elapsed time to recover average power. This makes RAPL useful for host-side work, provided the account identifies the domains actually reported and handles counter rollover. Package and subdomain counters must not be added when their coverage overlaps. RAPL does not measure discrete GPU energy, and access can require elevated permissions.

NVIDIA GPUs expose power information through the NVIDIA Management Library, accessible via the nvidia-smi command-line tool or programmatic bindings. GPU power monitoring must distinguish an instantaneous reading from a sensor-averaged value; the reported behavior depends on the device and query. Integrating samples requires a cadence and workload interval appropriate to that averaging window. Sampling an averaged signal more frequently does not recover short power spikes. Supported accumulated-energy counters can simplify interval accounting, but their scope and resolution still need checking, and neither method supplies full facility energy by itself.

Edge devices and microcontrollers often lack built-in power counters and require external instrumentation. Select current range, voltage measurement, sampling rate, and analog bandwidth to capture the workload’s active and sleep intervals. Board-level monitors can integrate inference energy; resolving shorter events requires adequate instrument bandwidth and careful treatment of filtering and aliasing. A fast sample rate alone cannot attribute energy to individual operations.

Mobile platform energy profiling

On mobile platforms, measurement depends on how much subsystem attribution the host operating system exposes. Some platforms report per-rail or per-component power for the CPU, GPU, neural processing unit, and radio, which lets a developer see which subsystem dominates total draw. Others expose only relative energy-impact scores or thermal states, which are useful for spotting regressions during development but cannot support an absolute energy claim. Vendor profilers that correlate power traces with kernel execution sit between these extremes. The design question is whether the reported quantity covers the electrical boundary the comparison needs, not which tool to use. A relative score cannot be converted into joules per inference. When it cannot, external instrumentation is required. Edge power measurement instruments differ by measurement role, and each role requires its own checks (table 4).

Edge measurement methodology

Edge energy measurements are useful only when they reflect the deployed duty cycle rather than an idealized active-only inference burst, so reproducible evaluation requires experimental controls matched to that workload. Simultaneous current and voltage logging must span both the active and quiescent states being measured. It captures rail voltage fluctuations rather than inferring power from current alone. Thermal and frequency stabilization is necessary for sustained-load comparisons, whereas cold starts and periodic bursts must be measured across their actual state transitions. Because edge devices spend most of their deployment life waiting between events, realistic duty-cycle accounting records both peak inference draw and time-averaged power across typical idle intervals. Component attribution may isolate or subtract peripheral loads to expose accelerator consumption. A deployment estimate, however, must restore the radios, sensors, and other subsystems needed to deliver the service. For duty cycle accounting, equation 8 expresses this relationship between active and idle power:

\[\AveragePower= \ActiveInferencePower\times \InferenceDutyCycle + \StandbyPower\times (1 - \InferenceDutyCycle) \tag{8}\]

where \(\InferenceDutyCycle\) is the duty cycle (fraction of time performing inference).

System-level energy profiling

Comprehensive energy accounting requires combining chip-level measurements with infrastructure overhead. The component terms must be disjoint: GPU-board telemetry that already includes device memory must not be added to another measurement of that same memory. They must also cover the IT boundary, with storage and server support loads included in the relevant measured terms. Equation 9 formalizes total energy as the sum of those component contributions scaled by facility overhead:

\[\FacilityEnergy= (\CpuEnergy+ \GpuEnergy+ \MemorySubsystemEnergy+ \NetworkEnergy) \times \text{PUE} \tag{9}\]

No single counter spans the full energy path, so system-level profilers like Intel VTune, NVIDIA Nsight Systems, and open-source tools such as PowerJoular aggregate measurements across components. On-chip counters estimate device power and may differ in sampling rate, coverage, and calibration; a rack power distribution unit (PDU) provides an independent measurement of the downstream IT load. A rack PDU does not measure central chillers, pumps, lighting, or upstream conversion losses, so facility energy must instead come from facility meters or from an explicitly time-matched PUE applied to the measured IT load. The same rule holds for power: facility power is the measured IT power, the sum of server, network, and storage equipment power, multiplied by the PUE of equation 2, so the overhead enters once at the facility boundary.

Development-time carbon tracking matters when it feeds placement and complexity decisions rather than becoming a report after the fact. Tools such as CarbonTracker (Anthony et al. 2020) and CodeCarbon (Schmidt et al. 2021) wrap the workload boundary, estimate energy from hardware counters or utilization models, attach the local grid intensity, and record the resulting emissions alongside the experiment metadata. The important systems pattern is not the API call but the timing of the measurement: the estimate must appear while model size, training duration, region, and schedule are still adjustable, before the run has already consumed its energy. Even then, facility energy is still not a complete footprint, because converting it to emissions requires a grid factor and the hardware that drew it carries manufacturing emissions that no power meter records.

Anthony, L. F. W., B. Kanding, and R. Selvan. 2020. Carbontracker: Tracking and Predicting the Carbon Footprint of Training Deep Learning Models. ICML Workshop on Challenges in Deploying and monitoring Machine Learning Systems.
Self-Check: Question

  1. An attention kernel has an arithmetic intensity of 3 FLOP/byte. An illustrative chip-and-memory energy model assigns 10 pJ per FLOP and 160 pJ per byte moved from DRAM, excluding static and facility energy. Which intervention directly targets the larger modeled energy contribution?

    1. Replacing the accelerator with one offering double the peak TFLOP/s while retaining the same memory bandwidth and memory technology.
    2. Increasing arithmetic precision from FP16 to FP32 to execute more FLOPs per memory transaction.
    3. Fusing normalization and attention operators and tiling intermediate activations to keep data in on-chip SRAM, reducing DRAM traffic.
    4. Lowering facility PUE from 1.5 to 1.1 to eliminate memory stall cycles.

  2. A 2 MW IT load cluster improves its Power Usage Effectiveness (PUE) from 1.58 to 1.10 without altering any model weights or server SKUs. Calculate the annual electricity savings in megawatt-hours (MWh) assuming 24/7 continuous operation, and explain why facility optimization is considered a first-order sustainability lever.

  3. In the CMOS dynamic power equation \(P_{\text{dynamic}}=\alpha_{\text{sw}}CV^2f\), the parameter \(\alpha_{\text{sw}}\) is the ____, summarizing capacitance-weighted switching per clock cycle under the chosen counting convention.

  4. An engineer needs to profile the energy consumption of a sub-watt microcontroller executing an on-device wake-word detection model that spends 98 percent of its time in deep sleep drawing 30 \(\mu\text{W}\). The microcontroller lacks on-chip energy counters. Which measurement approach is appropriate?

    1. Sampling the Linux RAPL interface at 100 Hz to capture CPU package power states.
    2. Querying NVML/nvidia-smi programmatic bindings to integrate instantaneous milliwatt metrics.
    3. Multiplying model parameter count by a theoretical constant of 5 pJ per parameter.
    4. Using a calibrated external analyzer with verified current range, analog bandwidth, synchronized voltage measurement, and integration windows covering sleep, active execution, and transitions.

  5. True or False: To determine the total environmental energy impact of an AI workload, an engineering team can simply sum the measured energy of the GPUs, CPUs, DRAM, and network interface cards reported by hardware telemetry.

  6. Explain how the Energy-Delay Product (\(\text{EDP} = E \times t\)) guides operating point selection in TinyML systems, and identify how voltage scaling interacts with clock frequency to minimize EDP.
