15.6 Data Center Energy and Resource Consumption

A training cluster can fit its accelerator power budget yet exceed its facility budget once support systems and cooling enter the account. Whether a facility can host the cluster at all is a feasibility question; the sustainability question is whether a change in power delivery or cooling reduces lifecycle cost while preserving that feasible operating envelope. The relevant comparison includes annual energy, local water use, equipment lifetime, and the energy wasted when cooling failures interrupt useful work.

Facility sustainability therefore has to be read as a chain of constraints rather than as a single PUE number. Persistent megawatt demand sets the grid and emissions exposure. Power delivery determines how much electricity becomes useful IT load. Cooling sets the facility overhead and the water drawn to remove heat. Water use then determines whether a technically efficient site is locally sustainable. The physical mechanisms below matter here because each assigns losses or resource use to a different accounting boundary.

15.6.1 Data center energy and AI workloads

Even when an engineer halves the energy of a single model, campus electricity demand still climbs if new data-center capacity and serving traffic expand faster than per-job efficiency. At facility scale, the optimization target is no longer a single model in isolation but the aggregate demand, facility overhead, and grid context surrounding every watt of IT power. The same IEA Energy and AI analysis behind the 2030 electricity projection cited at the chapter’s opening also tracks emissions, a different quantity. It estimated emissions from data-center electricity use at about 180 Mt at the time of the analysis and projected roughly 300 Mt by 2035 in its Base Case, still below 1.5 percent of total energy-sector emissions (International Energy Agency 2025).12 The energy burden grows with data-center capacity, training workloads, and inference demand (Patterson et al. 2021), so component efficiency must be evaluated against fleet expansion (Thompson et al. 2021; Dodge et al. 2022).

12 Data center emissions scale: Those emissions come from electricity demand that IEA’s 2025 Energy and AI analysis tracks separately. Data centers consumed about 1.5 percent of global electricity (415 TWh) in 2024, a share projected to approach 3 percent by 2030, with AI contributing to demand growth (International Energy Agency 2025). The largest hyperscale facilities can draw over 100 MW continuously, equivalent to powering tens of thousands of homes.

International Energy Agency. 2025. Energy and AI.
Thompson, N. C., K. Greenewald, K. Lee, and G. F. Manso. 2021. “Deep Learning’s Diminishing Returns: The Cost of Improvement Is Becoming Unsustainable.” IEEE Spectrum 58 (10): 50–55. https://doi.org/10.1109/mspec.2021.9563954.
Dodge, Jesse, Taylor Prewitt, Remi Tachet des Combes, Erika Odmark, Roy Schwartz, Emma Strubell, Alexandra Sasha Luccioni, Noah A. Smith, Nicole DeCario, and Will Buchanan. 2022. “Measuring the Carbon Intensity of AI in Cloud Instances.” 2022 ACM Conference on Fairness Accountability and Transparency, 1877–94. https://doi.org/10.1145/3531146.3533234.

13 Hyperscale data center footprint: A hyperscale campus makes long-lived investments in substations and transmission connections. Algorithmic improvements can reduce demand but do not remove the costs already incurred for that capacity. Supply contracts and operating schedules remain adjustable, subject to their terms and the physical connection.

The relevant facility quantity is persistent megawatt-class load, not the model name alone. Hyperscale campuses house large fleets of accelerator servers and hold loads of 100 MW or more for years at a time.13 A newer accelerator lowers a job’s energy only when that workload realizes the efficiency gain. Even then, lower energy per job need not reduce campus demand if the fleet uses the released capacity to perform more work.

Whether that demand keeps growing depends on the race between workload growth and efficiency. Masanet et al. (2020) show that efficiency gains held global data-center energy nearly flat through the 2010s even as computing output grew several-fold. Sustained AI demand growth can erode that offset, which is why the IEA projection cited earlier rises rather than remaining flat.

Masanet, Eric, Arman Shehabi, Nuoa Lei, Sarah Smith, and Jonathan Koomey. 2020. “Recalibrating Global Data Center Energy-Use Estimates.” Science 367 (6481): 984–86. https://doi.org/10.1126/science.aba3758.

Beyond computational demands, cooling can account for 30–40 percent of facility energy in the conventional air-cooled data-center designs surveyed by Ebrahimi et al. (2014). This is a design-regime range, not a constant for modern hyperscale or liquid-cooled facilities; section 15.6.3 derives the site-specific overhead through PUE and the cooling architecture.

The United States alone illustrates how cloud and AI infrastructure can reshape national energy planning. US data center electricity consumption more than tripled from 58 TWh in 2014 to 192 TWh in 2024, reaching 4.7 percent of national supply, according to the Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory (LBNL) estimates that figure 8 plots (Shehabi et al. 2024; Smith et al. 2026). LBNL’s reference case projects 649 TWh for 2030, about 11.8 percent of US electricity, inside a scenario range of 521 to 843 TWh (Smith et al. 2026). These scenarios describe possible demand, not a measured capacity ceiling. They motivate evaluating efficiency gains alongside the generation, transmission, and connection capacity needed to serve that demand.

Every kilowatt-hour in those national totals begins as electricity delivered to a rack and leaves as heat that must be removed from it. Delivering 120 kW to a single cabinet (the rated draw of one GB200 NVL72 rack) starts at the substation, and every conversion stage between the grid and the chip takes its share along the way.

15.6.2 Power delivery

Capacity planning fails when engineers budget only accelerator TDP, because transformers, uninterruptible power supplies, rack distribution, and voltage regulators each dissipate part of the incoming grid power as heat before a single watt reaches the silicon. Power and thermal envelopes traced that electrical path from the utility feed to the voltage regulator, and equation 2 measures the facility overhead along it. For a sustainability account, the question is where along that path energy and reliability margin are lost, and which boundary carries each loss. The first constraint sits outside the building: large grid-supplied ML facilities need sufficient substation and utility-interconnection capacity. Internal distribution improvements cannot raise that import limit, and on-site generation or storage adds a separate supply whose capacity, duration, and reliability must enter the design.

Substation lead time can run 4× longer than GPU procurement.

Systems Perspective 15.2: The interconnection queue
Deploying 10,000 GPUs and deploying the electrical substation required to power their IT load (9.6 MW, after the 7 MW GPU subtotal is expanded for rack support power) operate on different timelines. In this planning comparison, GPU procurement takes 6 months, while permitting, engineering, procurement, construction, and grid connection for a new 10+ MW substation take 24 months. The infrastructure path is therefore 4× longer than the accelerator path. Actual schedules depend on the site, utility, equipment availability, and approvals, so the ratio illustrates a dependency rather than a universal lead time.

Systems insight: In this comparison, power delivery determines when the fleet can begin useful work even if the accelerators arrive on schedule. The US generation-and-storage interconnection queue is distinct from a data center’s load-connection request; neither queue total directly measures available capacity for a new ML facility. Procurement must therefore follow a site-specific power-delivery schedule. Otherwise, delivered silicon can remain “electrically stranded” while the facility waits for its connection or transformer.

Inside the facility, every conversion stage turns part of the delivered power into heat. Along the path traced in Power and thermal envelopes (The Power Delivery Path), the utility transformer loses about 1–2 percent, an online double-conversion uninterruptible power supply (UPS) 3–5 percent (or 1–2 percent in eco mode), the power distribution unit (PDU) 2–3 percent, and baseboard voltage regulation 5–8 percent. Compounded, these stages dissipate roughly 9–17 percent of grid power before it reaches a transistor, and server power supplies add their own conversion loss on top. The accounting boundary decides where each loss appears. Losses upstream of the IT boundary, such as the transformer, UPS, and facility distribution, raise PUE. Server power supplies and baseboard voltage regulators sit inside the measured IT load, and their heat must be removed by the same cooling system that serves the accelerators. Higher-voltage distribution is one of the few design changes that shrinks this overhead directly, because the same power at a higher voltage needs less current, and resistive distribution losses \(\ConductorCurrent^2\ElectricalResistance\) fall with the square of that current. The 48 V rack distribution of Power delivery networks and transient dynamics applies that principle, and its realized saving depends on conductor design and the added conversion stages.

At ML rack power densities, the difference between 95 percent and 98 percent distribution efficiency is meaningful. Using 33 kW as a reference rack load, a first-order estimate applies the difference between those efficiencies as 3 percent of that load, yielding approximately 0.99 kW per rack. Across 300 racks, the estimated reduction is 297 kW, equivalent to roughly 36 GPU nodes or 9 four-node racks under this power mix. Sustaining that reduction over a 3-year lifecycle at $0.07/kWh gives estimated electricity savings of ~$546,361. An exact fixed-load comparison divides delivered power by each efficiency before subtracting input powers. The released capacity can support more work or lower consumption; spending it on additional nodes does not preserve the same electricity saving.

The Rack sizes the power envelope of a four-node DGX H100 rack; the same rack also shows which parts of a budget are IT load and which are facility overhead. A rack-attributed power budget itemizes more than GPU TDP (table 7). In this scenario, host, fabric, and equipment-side conversion losses belong to the IT load and sum to the IT subtotal; the row below it is an illustrative non-IT allocation at the stated PUE. A facility calculation multiplies the IT subtotal by its own PUE rather than reusing that allocation, and facility-side conversion losses are covered by PUE and must not be added again.

Evaluating DGX H100 power allocation (table 7) reveals that 32 GPUs at 700 W each deliver 22.4 kW. Host, fabric, and conversion losses, however, raise the IT subtotal to 30.8 kW, about 38 percent above the GPU subtotal. That IT subtotal is the load to multiply by a facility’s PUE; the table’s final row is one such allocation, which brings the rack-attributed total to about 50 percent above the GPU subtotal. Infrastructure planners who size their facility based on GPU TDP alone will underestimate the electrical load and may discover during commissioning that their power capacity is insufficient.

Synchronized training also changes the shape of the load. The correlated power swings introduced with grid dynamics (section 15.3.2) now appear inside the delivery chain, where coordinated computation and communication phases align across thousands of accelerators. Power delivery networks and transient dynamics analyzes how local decoupling, voltage regulators, and UPS controls filter these synchronous transients before they reach the utility connection. The same section estimates the aggregate ramp, in gigawatts per second, when a training cluster steps from communication to computation in unison. That estimate describes a load-side scenario rather than a measured utility input, and its sustainability consequence appears at the interconnection. Rack- or facility-level energy buffering (section 15.3.2), sized to the measured energy deficit rather than to a fixed allowance per rack, lets the grid see a smoother load. A well-characterized, controllable load is easier both to connect and to schedule against grid conditions.

Checkpoint 15.3: Power delivery physics

Verify the data center power path:

At the largest scales, the data center’s power draw represents a significant fraction of the local electrical grid’s capacity. A 100,000-GPU cluster, roughly a hundred times the book’s 1,024-GPU reference cluster, at 700 W per GPU has a 70 MW accelerator subtotal. Rack-profile support power, however, raises the IT load to 96.2 MW before PUE. With PUE, the total facility draw approaches 107.8 MW, the load of a small city. Such installations require dedicated feeds from the electrical grid, often with purpose-built substations and transmission lines. The substation lead time of 24 months in the interconnection-queue perspective above makes power availability one of the longest-lead-time constraints in building ML infrastructure.

At large-cluster scale, the same delivery limit becomes a grid-procurement problem rather than an electricity bill. Later large systems often lack energy disclosures comparable to the chapter’s opening anchor, which is precisely why the measurement contract requires workload telemetry instead of relying on published model documentation alone. This grid-procurement problem worsens because compute-optimal allocation (Compute-optimal resource allocation) grows parameters and training tokens together, so training work grows roughly with the square of model size, and energy follows it unless realized FLOPs per joule improve.

The electricity consumed by a single large-model training campaign can become nonnegligible relative to the output of a power plant. A 100 MW training facility operating at full capacity for one year consumes 876 GWh. A 100 MW wind farm cannot match that figure (at 30 percent capacity factor, a wind farm produces about 262.8 GWh per year, so the training facility would require the equivalent of approximately 3.3 large wind farms). Once power is available, its carbon intensity determines the emissions attributed to each megawatt-hour under the chosen accounting method, and at facility scale that factor becomes a siting decision that persists for the life of the building. Low-carbon electricity can coincide with favorable prices, but price and emissions factor are separate tests that a siting comparison must evaluate alongside power availability and the workload’s location constraints.

15.6.3 Cooling energy, heat rejection, and water

Nearly every watt of electrical power that enters a server cabinet leaves as heat, so a dense rack acts as a continuous heater whose thermal output must be removed at the rate it consumes electricity or the silicon throttles and shuts down. Cooling class and heat removal established how air and liquid loops carry that heat and which cooling class a given rack density requires; for a sustainability account, the question is what rejecting that heat to the outside environment costs in additional facility electricity and local water.

PUE (equation 2) captures facility fans, chillers, and facility-side conversion losses as overhead on top of the IT load. If facility cooling consumes 3–8 percent of IT power, that contribution alone adds 0.03–0.08 to PUE, and facility-side electrical losses and other non-IT loads add to it. Server fans, by contrast, lie within the IT load itself, so their energy sits in the denominator as well as the facility total, and reducing server-fan power changes the IT load rather than producing the same PUE reduction. Cooling choices must therefore be compared using total facility energy for the same useful work, not PUE alone.

The cooling class of Cooling class and heat removal settles thermodynamic feasibility, that the proposed cooling medium maintains die temperatures and component serviceability under peak workload. Lifecycle economics then compares installed capital expenditure, pumping and fan parasitics, water consumption, maintenance overhead, and equipment lifespan under the same computational load. A higher-density liquid-cooled installation may reduce operational energy, but its capital payback period cannot be inferred from rack power alone.

Napkin Math 15.7: The cooling overhead
Consider a 1,024-GPU cluster of H100s at 700 W each. To isolate the cooling choice, this simplified calculation treats accelerator power as the entire IT load and assigns all non-IT overhead to cooling; a procurement estimate must restore host, memory, network, and storage support before applying PUE.

  • IT power: 1,024 \(\times\) 700 W = 716.8 kW
  • Overhead removed: with the \((\text{PUE}-1)\) overhead arithmetic of notebook 15.3, moving from air cooling (PUE 1.55) to liquid cooling (PUE 1.08) removes 716.8 kW \(\times\) 0.47 = 336.9 kW of continuous power.

Savings: At $0.07/kWh, the annual savings are approximately $206,585. Over a 3-year hardware lifecycle, the cooling savings alone total $619,754, which is the figure to set against the installed capital cost of the liquid-cooling system when computing its payback period.

The final link in the cooling chain is heat rejection: getting heat from the facility water loop to the outside environment. A cooling tower sprays warm water over a fill medium, packing that spreads the water into thin films to enlarge its contact with the air, and uses evaporation to carry heat into the atmosphere. Evaporating a kilogram of water removes its latent heat of vaporization, 2,260 kJ/kg. Sensible heating instead depends on both the specific heat capacity, 4.18 kJ/kg/K, and the permitted temperature rise. Evaporation can therefore carry substantial heat with a small mass flow, but the evaporated water must be replaced.

A 10 MW data center with evaporative cooling towers can consume well over 100 million liters of water per year, depending on climate and cooling design, which has become a significant concern in water-stressed regions. At the evaporative WUE of 1.8 L/kWh used earlier, continuous operation consumes about 158 million liters per year, roughly 350–415 household-years of water use under the earlier annual household baseline. As ML data centers grow to 100 MW and beyond, their water footprint becomes a meaningful factor in local resource planning.

Dry coolers use fans to blow air over a radiator without evaporating cooling water. They avoid that direct water consumption, but the added fan electricity carries its own water burden at the generating plant, in a different watershed from the facility (figure 9). Heat rejection depends on the temperature difference between coolant and ambient air, so hot weather can reduce capacity or require more fan power. Hybrid designs use dry cooling when conditions permit and evaporative assistance when needed, trading water consumption against electrical demand and thermal margin.

Waste heat reuse treats thermal output as a resource. A district heating connection supplies a municipal heat network, while industrial coupling directs recoverable heat toward a compatible process. The benefit depends on the temperature, timing, and distance of the receiving demand, along with any pumping or heat-pump energy needed to supply it. Useful recovery can displace another heat source, although it does not eliminate the fleet’s electrical consumption14 (Ebrahimi et al. 2014). A liquid-cooling loop returning water at 50–65 degrees Celsius supplies low-grade heat that may serve nearby district heating, greenhouses, or other suitable sinks. Usability depends on the required supply temperature, seasonal demand, and transport losses. Because municipal heating networks often require supply temperatures above 70 degrees Celsius, the facility may need an electric heat pump to raise the coolant temperature before delivery. If an illustrative 10 MW thermal stream has an 80–90 percent recovery fraction, 8–9 MW is recovered before accounting for heat-pump electricity. Heat-pump output includes that electrical input and must be accounted for separately. Crediting useful heat does not reduce PUE below 1.0 because PUE compares total facility electrical input against IT electrical input, and exporting heat downstream does not reduce the electricity the building draws from the grid. Exported heat is instead reported through energy reuse effectiveness (ERE), which subtracts the reused energy from total facility energy before dividing by IT energy, or through a separate net-energy balance.

14 PUE boundary: The facility-to-IT ratio glossed with the opening energy boundary (The Green Grid 2007), formalized in section 15.4.1.3, says nothing about where recovered heat goes. Lowering non-IT overhead improves facility efficiency, but the metric does not capture the carbon intensity of electricity or the water consumed for cooling. Carbon usage effectiveness and water usage effectiveness therefore expose constraints that PUE alone leaves invisible.

The Green Grid. 2007. Green Grid Data Center Power Efficiency Metrics: PUE and DCIE. The Green Grid.
Ebrahimi, K., G. F. Jones, and A. S. Fleischer. 2014. “A Review of Data Center Cooling Technology, Operating Conditions and the Corresponding Low-Grade Waste Heat Recovery Opportunities.” Renewable and Sustainable Energy Reviews 31: 622–38. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.rser.2013.12.007.

The viability of waste heat reuse depends on the proximity of heat consumers. Urban data centers, despite their higher land and electricity costs, are often better positioned for waste heat reuse than remote facilities because they are close to residential and commercial heating loads. The result is a counterintuitive economic optimization: a data center in a Nordic city may have higher electricity costs but lower net operating costs after waste heat revenues, compared to a remote facility with cheaper electricity but no heat consumers nearby.

A cooling failure also costs energy, because the run repeats the work done since its last checkpoint. Rerunning 30 to 60 minutes on all 10,000 accelerators of a training cluster at rated power repeats about 3.5–7 MWh of accelerator energy, before host and facility overhead. Power drawn by idle accelerators during the outage adds to that total. ML goodput discounts the repeated work, but the energy account still pays for it (Facility reliability and environmental controls develops the cooling failure modes and their blast radius, and Checkpointing and Progress Preservation develops the checkpoint recovery that bounds the rollback). Together, power-delivery losses and heat rejection set the facility tax on every kilowatt-hour entering the building; how that electricity budget divides between one-time model training and continuous production serving determines where workload-level optimization must focus next.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A cloud provider can procure and install 10,000 GPUs within six months, but building the required electrical substation and securing utility interconnect approval takes 24 months. Explain why electrical power delivery represents the binding bottleneck for data center expansion, and state the systems consequence of this lead-time disparity.

  2. A facility audit lists conversion losses in five places: the utility transformer, the online UPS, the facility power distribution units, the server power supplies, and the baseboard voltage regulators. Which losses raise the facility’s PUE?

    1. The transformer, UPS, and facility distribution losses, because they lie upstream of the IT boundary; server power supplies and voltage regulators sit inside the measured IT load.
    2. All five, because every conversion loss turns delivered power into heat that the cooling system must remove.
    3. Only the server power supplies and voltage regulators, because they sit closest to the accelerators that do the useful work.
    4. None of them, because PUE measures cooling overhead and conversion losses are electrical rather than thermal.

  3. Facility A reports a Power Usage Effectiveness (PUE) of 1.08 on an electrical grid with carbon intensity of \(450\text{ g CO}_2/\text{kWh}\). Facility B reports PUE 1.35 on a grid with \(50\text{ g CO}_2/\text{kWh}\). For a \(10\text{ MW}\) IT compute load, calculate the total hourly electrical power drawn and carbon emissions for both facilities, and explain the limitation of using PUE as a sole environmental metric.

  4. A 10 MW facility in a water-stressed region can reject heat through evaporative cooling towers or through dry coolers. Explain the water and energy trade-off between the two, and why the operator might accept higher fan energy.

See Answers →

15.7 Training vs. Inference Energy Analysis

The facility account sets the overhead on every kilowatt-hour, but the total magnitude of the load depends on whether the fleet is executing a concentrated training run or a globally distributed inference workload. Training a large language model is a concentrated, highly visible energy event that consumes thousands of megawatt-hours over weeks or months. Serving the same model to a billion daily queries is the opposite shape of load: energy spent continuously, query by query, year after year. Understanding where the majority of the energy budget goes dictates where optimization efforts must concentrate.

Training and inference share opportunities to reduce computation, data movement, and idle power, but their success criteria differ. Training must reach the target model quality within its completion budget; inference must serve the required demand at the agreed quality and latency. The lifecycle energy account identifies which phase contributes most, while those constraints determine which interventions can reduce its cost without sacrificing useful work.

15.7.1 Carbon benchmarks across AI workloads

Two published carbon estimates for the same neural architecture search can differ by orders of magnitude when one assumes older GPUs, an average facility PUE, and a fossil-heavy grid (Strubell et al. 2019) while the other measures specialized accelerators in an efficient, low-carbon facility (Patterson et al. 2021).15 Even though the IEA estimate cited earlier puts emissions from data-center electricity use at about 180 Mt, below 1.5 percent of total energy-sector emissions, workload-level carbon benchmarks govern where engineering teams invest optimization effort, and those benchmarks are only as reliable as the lifecycle boundaries behind them.

15 Neural architecture search (NAS) carbon cost: The ~284,000 kg CO2e figure from Strubell et al. (2019) represents an unoptimized Evolved Transformer search that drops sharply when accounting for TPU hardware, grid mix, and proxy tasks (Patterson et al. 2021). The systems lesson is boundary discipline: search, tuning, and failed trials belong inside the carbon accounting boundary, but reusable architecture search should be amortized across all downstream deployments.

Strubell, Emma, Ananya Ganesh, and Andrew McCallum. 2019. “Energy and Policy Considerations for Deep Learning in NLP.” Proceedings of the 57th Annual Meeting of the Association for Computational Linguistics, 3645–50. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/p19-1355.
Patterson, David, Joseph Gonzalez, Quoc Le, Chen Liang, Lluis-Miquel Munguia, Daniel Rothchild, David So, Maud Texier, and Jeff Dean. 2021. “Carbon Emissions and Large Neural Network Training.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2104.10350.

The GPT-3 calculation in notebook 15.1 put one training run at hundreds of trans-Atlantic passenger round trips. Such run-level comparisons convey scale, but they do not by themselves describe the footprint of a deployed service. The operational lesson is that carbon accounting must distinguish training from recurring inference over a declared observation period. Meta’s recommendation workloads (RM-1 through RM-5) split operational emissions roughly evenly between training and inference, while inference dominates its language-model example (figure 10). The public baselines, including GPT-3 and BERT-NAS, report training only; their bars do not establish a lifetime training-to-inference ratio. For high-traffic Archetype B deployments (Archetype B (DLRM at Scale)), both recurring serving and model updates belong in the account before deciding which phase offers the larger reduction.

15.7.2 Training energy demands

Even the training-only bars in the comparison above represent massive, concentrated power events: as early as 2020, the supercomputer Microsoft built for OpenAI combined 285,000 CPU cores and 10,000 GPUs (Langston 2020) operating continuously for months, a dated reference point that subsequent frontier clusters have multiplied severalfold. Those intensive computational loads generate heat that cooling infrastructure must continuously remove, the facility overhead quantified in section 15.6.3, and reducing the draw requires co-optimizing hardware architecture, parallelism strategy, and algorithmic efficiency. Every single-run training account in this chapter, from the GPT-3 run to the 70B model of notebook 15.6, also prices only the winning configuration. Development multiplies each run, by 5× for the sweep in section 15.5.2, while periodic retraining during service repeats part of that cost. Training energy is therefore paid once per completed run but recurs across the model’s development and update cycle.

Langston, Jennifer. 2020. Microsoft Announces New Supercomputer, Lays Out Vision for Future AI Work. Microsoft Source.

Distributed systems energy optimization

Distributing a training job across accelerators introduces coordination costs alongside the energy spent on computation. Distributed parallelism strategies exchange gradients, parameters, or activations while synchronization stalls leave idle accelerators drawing baseline power.16 The resulting energy share cannot be inferred from cluster size alone because topology, collective algorithm, message volume, overlap, and utilization determine the account. Some AllToAll patterns also increase traffic faster than aggregate useful work as the participating set grows.

16 Parallelism energy overhead: Data, model, and pipeline parallelism impose distinct communication and idle-power costs. Data parallelism reduces gradients, tensor parallelism exchanges partial activations, and pipeline parallelism introduces bubbles. A valid comparison reaches the same model-quality target under the same completion constraint, then measures each strategy’s runtime and system energy, including communication and waiting. Fixing time-to-train by assumption would hide one of the effects the comparison needs to expose.

↩︎ Parallelism strategy selection and the communication cost of each strategy are established in Parallelism strategy selection and engineering trade-offs.

The Fleet Law (equation 1.3) turns this overhead into an energy account. If the fleet draws average power \(\AveragePower\) while executing one step on \(\DeviceCount\) accelerators, the energy of that step is approximately

\[\TrainingStepEnergy\approx \AveragePower\times \DistributedStepTime(\DeviceCount) = \AveragePower\left( \frac{\IronLawComputeTime}{\DeviceCount} + \CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount) + \SynchronizationTime(\DeviceCount) - \OverlappedTime \right) \tag{13}\]

The compute term accounts for local mathematical work; communication and synchronization make the distributed update possible. When these costs remain exposed, they extend the step while accelerators, hosts, and the fabric continue drawing power, adding powered time on the communication and coordination axes of C³. Their energy share depends on power during those intervals as well as on their duration. A quarter of the step spent in unhidden communication accounts for a quarter of its energy only if average fleet power during that interval equals the average over the whole step. Accelerators stalled at a collective can draw less than at full arithmetic load, so the share must be read from a power trace. Overlap, smaller messages, and fewer stragglers shorten the step, and they save energy to the extent that the shorter powered interval is not offset by higher average power or by extra work to reach the same model quality. The parallelism choices of Distributed Training are therefore sustainability choices as well.

↩︎ The data-movement boundaries that set what each transferred byte costs, from on-package links through intra-node crossbars to inter-node switches, are established in The Physics of Data Movement.

Beyond the communication term, coordinated resource management offers four levers with distinct effects on energy and emissions. At the cluster level, dynamic workload placement consolidates compatible jobs so genuinely unused hardware can enter lower-power sleep states. True savings must be verified at the facility meter rather than inferred from idle node counts. Carbon-aware scheduling (section 15.9.4) shifts flexible runs toward cleaner grid windows even when total energy use is unchanged, subject to data-movement overhead, completion deadlines, and reliability bounds. Inside active nodes, multi-tenant sharing raises accelerator compute and memory occupancy across concurrent tasks, though contention can lengthen execution and increase energy per completed job. Adaptive inference batching groups compatible requests to improve throughput within their latency limits. Changing training batch size instead requires checking the total work needed to reach the target model quality. Each lever must therefore be evaluated against completed useful work and its service constraints rather than occupancy alone, because energy spent on work that ML goodput discounts, the scheduling, runtime, and program losses of ML Goodput, still enters the energy account.

Energy-aware training frameworks reduce step energy directly rather than shifting where it is spent. Zeus improved training energy efficiency by 15.3–75.8 percent across its evaluated workloads by automatically finding energy-performance trade-offs (You et al. 2023), while Perseus reduced large-model training energy consumption by up to 30 percent by mitigating energy bloat (Chung et al. 2023). These tools make energy optimization available beyond hyperscale companies, yet all of these training costs stop accruing when the run stops. For high-volume services, the larger term is often the deployment that follows, as the crossover calculation in section 15.7.3 shows.

You, Jie, Jae-Won Chung, and Mosharaf Chowdhury. 2023. “Zeus: Understanding and Optimizing GPU Energy Consumption of DNN Training.” 20th USENIX Symposium on Networked Systems Design and Implementation (NSDI 23), 119–39.
Chung, Jae-Won, Yile Gu, Insu Jang, Luoxi Meng, Nikhil Bansal, and Mosharaf Chowdhury. 2023. “Reducing Energy Bloat in Large Model Training.” Proceedings of the ACM SIGOPS 30th Symposium on Operating Systems Principles abs/2312.06902: 144–59. https://doi.org/10.1145/3694715.3695970.

15.7.3 Inference energy costs

A model trained once can draw far more electricity over a year of high-volume serving than it ever consumed during training (Wu et al. 2022). Although a single inference request costs only a fraction of a watt-hour, serving executes every time the model answers a query, classifies an image, or scores a recommendation, scaling continuously with user traffic across search engines, recommendation systems, and generative AI services.

Cumulative inference emissions overtake the one-time training cost.

Consider a model serving 10 million queries per day at 0.001 kWh of attributed facility electricity per query, including its share of idle capacity and support systems. At this serving rate, daily energy consumption reaches 10,000 kWh (10 million queries \(\times\) 0.001 kWh), which accumulates to 3,650,000 kWh over a year of continuous service. Evaluated against the average US grid carbon intensity of 0.429 kg/kWh, this electricity demand produces 1,565.9 t (1,565,850 kg) of greenhouse gas emissions annually.

Compared with the earlier 7B/64-A100 training example (5.7 t), the smallest of the three training accounts and therefore the fastest to overtake, cumulative inference emissions exceed training emissions after approximately 1.3 days of deployment at this scale. The serving scenario and the training run describe different models, so the comparison does not trace one model’s lifecycle. It shows how quickly this serving volume would overtake a training run of that size. For larger training runs, the crossover can shift to weeks or months depending on query volume, per-query energy, and grid intensity. The lifecycle perspective therefore sets the priority: optimize inference efficiency for widely deployed models, and focus training efficiency efforts on models that undergo frequent retraining or experimental iteration.

Production measurements in figure 10 show why this crossover must remain workload-specific. Inference dominates the language-model account, while training and inference contribute roughly equally for the recommendation models. Training FLOPs alone omit serving costs, but the larger phase must be established from the service’s measured workload and observation period.

Inference workloads, like other software services, scale with demand. An always-available service consumes energy both while processing requests and while maintaining capacity for expected latency and availability. The sustainability account must include query volume, workload mix, and idle provisioned resources.

A production measurement shows how large that account is. Google instrumented the fleet serving its Gemini assistant and attributed energy to the median text prompt in May 2025 across every component of the serving stack (Elsworth et al. 2025). The full-stack figure is 0.24 Wh per prompt (figure 11), of which the active accelerators account for only 58 percent. The host processors and memory that feed them add 25 percent, and idle machines held for latency and availability together with facility overhead add another 17 percent. Counting only active accelerators on the most efficiently used machines, the boundary many published estimates use, yields 0.10 Wh, a 2.4× understatement of the same workload. The measured prompt also sits at about a quarter of the 1 Wh per query assumed in the worked example above, a reminder that per-query energy is a property of a specific model, serving stack, and utilization rather than a constant. The same study reports that software and utilization improvements cut the median prompt’s energy 33× in a single year, so a per-query figure also carries a date.

Checkpoint 15.4: The training-inference flip

Consider a vision model where training requires 2,000 GPU-hours at an average accelerator power draw of 300 W. Once deployed, the model serves 1 million requests per day for 730 days, with each request occupying an accelerator for 50 ms at an average draw of 100 W. Assume requests do not overlap on an accelerator. This active-only account excludes idle capacity, host systems, networking, and facility overhead.

The energy inefficiency of the decode phase

Prefill and decode stress distinct memory and arithmetic pathways, so meaningful energy comparisons require a declared serving workload. Prefill amortizes loaded weights across all prompt positions simultaneously, whereas each autoregressive decode iteration reuses weights only across active sequence batch slots to emit one token at a time (figure 12). Batch size, context length, precision, and the latency target dictate whether memory bandwidth or arithmetic execution limits realized utilization.

↩︎ Why small-batch decode is bound by memory bandwidth, and how batching reuses each weight load, is established in Batching across model architectures.

That split in dataflow governs per-token energy through data movement and arithmetic intensity. During prefill, parallel matrix operations reuse weights across prompt positions and can achieve high compute utilization when the workload exposes enough parallel work. During decode, each iteration produces one token per active sequence while accessing model parameters and an expanding KV cache. At small batches, this traffic can leave arithmetic units underused; batching improves weight reuse, subject to memory capacity and latency constraints.

The energy roofline of section 15.4.2.3 puts a number on that underuse. At batch size one, each decode step reads every FP16 weight (2 bytes) to perform two FLOPs with it, an arithmetic intensity of 1 FLOP/byte against an energy crossover of 16 FLOP/byte (equation 7). Under the chapter’s modeled coefficients, moving each parameter’s bytes from DRAM costs 320 pJ while the arithmetic on it costs 20 pJ. Movement is therefore 16× the arithmetic and about 94 percent of the modeled dynamic energy per generated token, before any KV-cache traffic is counted. The coefficient is the table’s off-package DRAM value; the high-bandwidth memory that feeds an accelerator costs less per byte (Energy cost of data movement), which narrows the ratio without reversing it at batch one. Batching \(\BatchSize\) sequences reuses each weight load \(\BatchSize\) times and raises the intensity to \(\BatchSize\) FLOP/byte, so the two terms balance only near a batch of 16. Batching is therefore the first energy lever for decode, bounded by memory capacity for the KV cache and by the latency target.

Static power waste occurs when powered circuitry waits rather than producing useful outputs. Reducing that waiting can improve energy efficiency, but a comparison must meter the same hardware boundary and preserve output quality, context, latency, and serving load. Prompt processing and autoregressive generation perform different work; equal token counts alone do not establish a fair energy ratio.

15.7.4 Edge execution and the fleet energy account

Moving inference from a cloud data center onto a billion phones or vehicles does not make its energy cost disappear; it shifts the draw into device batteries, local thermals, and handset manufacturing. Edge intelligence runs models on user devices or nearby computing nodes, avoiding raw-input transfers to a central facility while adding or extending computation at the edge. Local processing and duty cycling reduce total energy only when the communication and remote work they avoid exceed the device’s added computation and idle power. Device manufacturing joins that added cost when deployment requires new hardware, and the comparison holds task quality and service availability fixed.

↩︎ The power, memory, and compute budgets of edge devices, and how on-device training amplifies them, are established in Design Constraints.

Sudhakar, Soumya, Vivienne Sze, and Sertac Karaman. 2023. “Data Centers on Wheels: Emissions from Computing Onboard Autonomous Vehicles.” IEEE Micro 43 (1): 29–39. https://doi.org/10.1109/mm.2022.3219803.

Running inference at the edge does not eliminate energy concerns. In autonomous vehicles, time-critical perception and control must meet response deadlines despite variable network connectivity, so onboard computation remains part of the operating budget. Sudhakar et al. (2023) model the potential emissions of a widely deployed autonomous-vehicle fleet, showing how device power and operating time can accumulate at global scale; their comparison concerns fleet-level scenarios, not an assertion that each vehicle consumes as much energy as a data center.

Smartphones, wearables, and sensors follow the same accounting. Aggregate operational energy depends on the number of devices, their power states, and the time spent in each state, so a small average draw per device can still produce a large fleet total. Attributing that total to AI requires separating the additional workload from the device’s other functions. Device power budgets constrain both local inference and learning, so the comparison must also account for duty cycling, selective cloud escalation, and the repeated computation and model transfers that federated training requires.

Edge and mobile power budgets

Edge devices operate within battery, thermal, and supply limits that differ from those of data center accelerators. These limits follow from the deployment rather than the processor’s instruction set. The engineering choice is to match each inference workload to a device that meets latency and accuracy requirements within its sustained power and energy budgets.

Power budgets reflect the physical constraints of battery capacity, thermal dissipation, and deployment environment. These constraints propagate differently across the edge AI power budget categories, from microcontrollers to vehicles (table 8). TinyML devices (microcontrollers that run small models in kilobytes of memory) must fit average demand to battery-life or harvesting targets while also supporting peak draw. Mobile devices must balance user experience with battery life, and automotive systems face thermal constraints within enclosed vehicle compartments despite having access to vehicle power.

TinyML power state dynamics

TinyML devices are the smallest members of edge fleets, sitting below the 2–10 W phones of Archetype C (Federated MobileNet) in both memory and power. Their energy efficiency is a sustainability concern because billions of edge inference calls add up to a measurable carbon footprint at fleet scale. TinyML efficiency depends heavily on duty cycling, where devices alternate between deep sleep and active inference. The duty-cycle model of equation 8 applies directly, with \(\InferenceDutyCycle = \InferenceActiveTime/\DutyCyclePeriod\) and the deep-sleep draw in place of idle power.

↩︎ The same duty-cycled power budget, applied to on-device learning rather than inference, is established in Continuous training orchestration.

Consider a keyword-spotting model on a Cortex-M4 microcontroller, an extreme case that obeys the same battery arithmetic as the Archetype C phones. The device draws 15 mW of active inference power for 20 ms in each detection cycle, falls to a deep-sleep draw of 10 microamps at 3.3 V (33 µW) between evaluations, and repeats on a 1 s detection period during continuous listening. Substituting these values into equation 8 yields the average power draw:

\[P_{\text{avg}} = 15\text{ mW} \times 0.02 + 0.033\text{ mW} \times 0.98 = 0.30 + 0.032 \approx 0.33\text{ mW}\]

A 250 mAh coin cell at 3 V nominal stores 750 mWh, enough for approximately 2,260 hours, about 94 days, under this idealized budget. The estimate covers compute only. Continuous listening also requires powered sensing and input buffering while the microcontroller sleeps, and their energy, regulator losses, and battery derating must enter any deployment estimate.

On-device learning and the battery wall

Gradient-based on-device learning costs more work and memory than inference alone. A dense gradient step needs roughly 2–3\(\times\) the forward arithmetic, with activation, gradient, and optimizer-state memory budgeted separately. The battery, thermal, and foreground-latency test that decides when a phone may admit such an update is established in Energy, heat, and background admission. Its representative phone envelope (sustained processing within 2–3 W and bursts to 5–10 W for limited periods) bounds how long an on-device update can run alongside battery energy, memory, and the update deadline. Processor TDP alone does not establish feasibility. The sustainability account adds the fleet-scale question of how much energy the admitted updates consume in total.

Napkin Math 15.8: The energy of learning
Problem: Consider fine-tuning a small language model (1B parameters) on a user’s smartphone overnight. Is this feasible within a 5 percent battery budget?

Math:

  1. Phone battery: Typical capacity is approximately 15 Wh, or about 54,000 J.
  2. Budget: 5 percent of 54,000 J = 2,700 J.
  3. Training cost: this step uses illustrative per-parameter energy coefficients for a mobile accelerator (assumed for this scenario, not measured on a particular phone). The backward pass costs twice the forward pass because it computes gradients with respect to both activations and weights, so one training step costs about three forward passes:
    • Forward pass: \(\approx\) 2 nJ/param.
    • Backward pass: \(\approx\) 4 nJ/param.
    • Total per token: 6 nJ/param \(\times 10^9\) params = 6 J/token.
  4. Capacity: 2,700 J / 6 J/token = 450 tokens.

Systems insight: The calculation establishes a token capacity, not whether the proposed adaptation fits. That decision also requires the token count needed to reach the quality target, the available memory, and sustained device power. If the required work exceeds the battery budget, parameter-efficient fine-tuning or sparse updates are candidates for reducing cost; their measured energy and achieved quality must establish whether they close the gap.

The local-processing decision compares the energy of the required computation with the communication and remote computation it avoids. Per-bit radio costs motivate the comparison, but the full job must also include repeated training rounds and model transfers.

Systems Perspective 15.3: The energy hierarchy

Trade-off: The architectural choice between processing data locally and sending it to the cloud is governed by an energy budget. The energy-to-communication ratio motivates comparing local work with transmission, but a decision requires the total energy of each alternative at the required quality and latency.

Energy cost per operation (approximate):

  • 32-bit integer add: 0.1 pJ
  • 32-bit float mult: 3.7 pJ
  • Wireless transmit (1 bit): 100,000–500,000 pJ (Bluetooth/Wi-Fi)

Systems insight: Under these illustrative component costs, a transmitted bit consumes far more energy than one arithmetic operation. The radio cost is quoted per bit; per byte it reaches roughly 0.8 to 4 million pJ, about two orders of magnitude above the lower bound given for the wired network row of table 2. This motivates local processing but does not establish an offloading threshold: include memory, radio startup and protocol overhead, idle power, and remote computation in the full edge-cloud comparison. Local processing wins only when those complete terms favor it.

Radio transmission dwarfs local arithmetic in energy.

Energy harvesting for autonomous edge AI

TinyML can operate from harvested ambient energy when the available supply supports the full device workload. Sustained operation requires both an adequate long-term energy budget and a way to handle intervals when harvesting falls below demand, through storage, duty cycling, or suspended execution.

Ambient energy harvesting delivers from about a microwatt to about ten milliwatts per device, depending on the source (table 9). A keyword spotting model optimized to 0.5 mW average power matches the nominal output of approximately 5 cm² of indoor solar harvesting only near the top of the listed illumination range, before conversion losses and other device loads. This average-power comparison does not establish uninterrupted operation. The design must cover sensing, storage losses, and periods of dim or absent light, or permit the workload to pause. Ambient supply removes the need for purchased operating electricity only when it covers that complete budget; manufacturing, storage, maintenance, and disposal still enter the lifecycle account.

Cascade inference architecture

Beyond individual device efficiency, architectural patterns determine total system energy consumption across edge-cloud boundaries. A cascade architecture deploys a task-appropriate small edge model to filter inputs before cloud inference. Equation 14 expresses total energy as the sum of local processing plus probabilistically-triggered cloud costs:

\[\CascadeInferenceEnergy= \LocalInferenceEnergy + \CascadeEscalationProbability\times (\CloudOffloadEnergy + \CloudInferenceEnergy) \tag{14}\]

where \(\CascadeEscalationProbability\) is the probability of requiring cloud inference (measured on the deployment input distribution).

Consider an illustrative visual inspection system where a lightweight edge model, such as MobileNetV3-Small, consumes 0.5 mJ per image classification, whereas a high-capacity cloud model, such as ResNet-152, requires 50 mJ per inference. Transmitting an image across a cellular uplink adds 10 mJ. If the edge model resolves routine cases locally and escalates only ambiguous inputs, for an escalation rate \(\CascadeEscalationProbability\) of 10 percent, substituting these parameters into equation 14 gives the average cascade energy:

\[E_{\text{cascade}} = 0.5 + 0.10 \times (10 + 50) = 0.5 + 6.0 = 6.5 \text{ mJ/image}\]

Compared to always-cloud inference at 60 mJ per image, this illustrative cascade reduces the modeled energy by 89 percent. Quality requires a separate test of local decisions, missed escalations, and the cloud model; the energy calculation says nothing about accuracy.

Wake-word triggered systems

Always-on systems can use hierarchical wake detection to reduce average power across stages of increasing capability and draw. In this illustrative model, an analog front end consumes 10 µW while monitoring for voice activity. A detected event activates a compact neural network wake detector drawing 100 µW. A positive wake decision then triggers full-model inference at 10 mW for 50 ms. The energy calculation uses those assumed state powers and event rates; a deployed system must also account for false activations, missed wake words, and transition costs when evaluating its energy and service quality.

↩︎ The always-on budget of wake-word detection, set by how often the detector evaluates over the listening period, is established in Where adaptation pays.

For an illustrative speech-active fraction of 5 percent and a wake-event rate of 0.001 per second (one event per 1,000 s), each full-model event lasts 0.05 s. The front end draws continuously, the wake detector draws only during speech, and the full model contributes its event rate times its event energy:

\[P_{\text{avg}} = 0.01 + 0.05 \times 0.1 + 0.001 \times 10 \times 0.05 = 0.0155\text{ mW}\]

The hierarchical approach achieves 15.5 µW average power compared to 10 mW for always-active full inference, a reduction of roughly 645× within this modeled boundary. Battery lifetime also requires battery capacity, conversion losses, sensors, and other device loads.

Federated learning energy analysis

Training at the edge keeps raw data local but replaces its transfer with repeated model downloads and client-update uploads. This is the regime of Archetype C (Federated MobileNet), where phones train a shared model within a sustained power budget of a few watts. Equation 15 contrasts the energy trade-offs between federated and centralized approaches. Here \(\FederatedRoundCount\) is the number of rounds, and \(\ParticipatingClientCount\) is the number of participating clients per round. The term \(\ClientUpdateUploadEnergy\) is one client-update upload, \(\RawDataUploadEnergy\) is one client’s raw-data upload, and \(\CloudTrainingRunEnergy\) is the energy of one centralized cloud training run:

\[\FederatedTrainingEnergy= \FederatedRoundCount\left[\ParticipatingClientCount\left(\ClientLocalTrainingEnergy+ \ModelDownloadEnergy+ \ClientUpdateUploadEnergy\right) + \ServerAggregationEnergy\right]\] \[\CentralizedTrainingEnergy= \ParticipatingClientCount\times \RawDataUploadEnergy+ \CloudTrainingRunEnergy \tag{15}\]

Federated learning becomes more energy-efficient only when repeated model communication, local training, and aggregation consume less energy than raw-data transfer and centralized training at comparable model quality. The comparison must cover the same client population and account for repeated participation across rounds. Data being larger than one model update is insufficient by itself because downloads and uploads recur. Privacy and energy benefits require separate evidence: keeping raw data local does not by itself prevent updates from revealing private information, and any protection mechanism adds its own costs to the energy account.

Training, serving, and edge deployment all draw operational energy, and none of these accounts includes the hardware that performs the work. That physical supply chain, from fabrication through disposal, is the lifecycle term that remains to be counted.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Which systems analogy and operational reality best explains why inference often dominates total lifecycle energy for widely deployed foundation models?

    1. Inference requires higher instantaneous wattage per accelerator than training because real-time serving disables dynamic voltage scaling.
    2. Training is like a one-time rocket launch that burns concentrated energy over several weeks, whereas inference is like operating a commercial airline fleet that burns energy continuously across billions of recurring user interactions over years.
    3. Inference models must perform a full backward pass on every user query to maintain accuracy, doubling computational cost.
    4. Inference cannot run on specialized accelerators, forcing cloud operators to use energy-inefficient legacy CPUs.

  2. A distributed training job expands from 1,024 to 2,048 accelerators. Explain which mechanisms can increase communication energy and what measurements establish its share of step energy for the actual run.

  3. Profiling an autoregressive LLM serving pipeline shows that the prefill phase achieves 55 percent Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU), while the token-generation decode phase achieves only 6 percent MFU with high HBM bandwidth utilization. What is the physical mechanism causing decode energy inefficiency?

    1. Decode executes in FP32 precision while prefill executes in INT4 precision.
    2. Prefill bypasses high-bandwidth memory entirely by storing all weights in CPU host RAM.
    3. Decode requires running backpropagation to generate key-value cache embeddings for each new token.
    4. Decode is memory-bandwidth bound: each generated token requires reading all model weights from HBM with low arithmetic intensity, leaving tensor execution units idling while drawing static leakage and clock distribution power.

  4. A mobile application team proposes running nightly on-device full backpropagation fine-tuning for a 1B-parameter model on user smartphones. Using the concept of the ‘battery wall’, explain how to assess a 5 percent overnight battery budget and identify alternatives if the measured workload exceeds it.

  5. A TinyML sensor runs inference for 10 ms at 20 mW active power and sleeps for 990 ms at 20 \(\mu\text{W}\) deep-sleep power during a 1-second monitoring period. Calculate the average power draw, and explain how duty cycling enables multi-year battery operation.

  6. Order the stages of a hierarchical wake-word cascade designed to minimize average energy consumption on a smart speaker from lowest power to highest power: (1) large language model or full automatic speech recognition running on host processor or cloud, (2) small convolutional or recurrent neural network wake-word classifier verifying the trigger phrase, (3) ultra-low-power Voice Activity Detection (VAD) circuit listening continuously at microwatt power.

15.8 Hardware Lifecycle and E-Waste

The environmental cost of an AI accelerator begins long before its first FLOP is calculated (Gupta et al. 2022; Luccioni et al. 2023; NVIDIA Corporation 2025; Schneider et al. 2025). The per-H100 manufacturing footprint quantified in section 15.5.5 is incurred before the device is ever powered on.17 A training fleet of a few thousand such accelerators therefore carries a manufacturing debt of the same order as the operational emissions of a GPT-3-scale training run on the US-average grid (about 552,123 kg). A comprehensive life cycle assessment (LCA) quantifies the cumulative environmental impact across four key phases: design, manufacture, use, and disposal. LCA can reveal manufacturing as a major share of lifecycle impact, making it a critical sustainability lever that operational efficiency improvements alone cannot address. The first published LCA of an AI accelerator, covering five generations of Google’s TPU across all four phases, introduced compute carbon intensity, the lifecycle emissions per unit of computation. Unlike performance per watt, the metric charges each chip for its own manufacturing, and by that measure the TPU v6e is about 3× better than the TPU v4i (Schneider et al. 2025).

Gupta, Udit, Young Geun Kim, Sylvia Lee, Jordan Tse, Hsien-Hsin S Lee, Gu-Yeon Wei, David Brooks, and Carole-Jean Wu. 2022. “Chasing Carbon: The Elusive Environmental Footprint of Computing.” IEEE Micro 42 (4): 37–47. https://doi.org/10.1109/MM.2022.3163226.
Schneider, Ian, Hui Xu, Stephan Benecke, David Patterson, Keguo Huang, Parthasarathy Ranganathan, and Cooper Elsworth. 2025. “Life-Cycle Emissions of AI Hardware: A Cradle-to-Grave Approach and Generational Trends.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2502.01671.

17 Life cycle assessment (LCA): Standardized by ISO 14040/14044, LCA traces environmental impact from raw material extraction through disposal (International Organization for Standardization 2006a, 2006b). For AI hardware, LCA separates embodied manufacturing emissions from operational energy use, making hardware refresh cycles and accelerator lifespan extension first-order sustainability levers that operational efficiency alone cannot substitute.

International Organization for Standardization. 2006a. ISO 14040:2006 Environmental Management – Life Cycle Assessment – Principles and Framework. ISO standard.
International Organization for Standardization. 2006b. ISO 14044:2006 Environmental Management – Life Cycle Assessment – Requirements and Guidelines. ISO standard.

These four phases organize the same emissions by time rather than by activity or reporting scope, as the second column of table 5 records, so a phase total must never be added to an activity or scope total. Evaluated across that full timeline, life cycle assessments can show that discarding functional hardware purely for modest efficiency gains causes more environmental harm through embodied carbon than it saves in operational power. The tipping point where new hardware becomes environmentally justified depends on utilization and grid intensity, a break-even worked in section 15.8.6.

Each of the four lifecycle stages adds its burden through a distinct physical mechanism (figure 13). System design fixes much of the lifetime load, manufacturing embeds carbon, water, and chemical burdens during fabrication, operation draws grid electricity for training and serving, and decommissioning produces electronic waste unless hardware is refurbished or its components recovered.

The lifecycle sequence connects design choices to costs incurred at different times. Experimentation consumes resources before deployment; manufacturing adds embodied carbon; and use couples the workload to grid and cooling constraints. Retirement creates a choice among reuse, recovery, and disposal. Extending useful service spreads the hardware’s embodied burden over more work, but it does not reverse emissions already incurred. The environmental benefit depends on the replacement it avoids and the operating cost of keeping the existing equipment in service.

15.8.1 Design and experimentation phase

Public training-energy figures almost always report the single final run that produced the published checkpoint, omitting the months of failed experiments, hyperparameter sweeps, and architecture searches that preceded it. Those preliminary runs belong to the design phase, which covers research, development, and optimization before deployment. Automated neural architecture search (NAS) can evaluate many configurations. Section 15.7.1 showed that two estimates of the same search can differ by orders of magnitude depending on whether the boundary includes proxy searches, full runs, data-center overhead, grid mix, and reuse across downstream models (Strubell et al. 2019; Patterson et al. 2021). Later efficient NAS methods use weight sharing, continuous relaxations, and hardware-aware search to reduce that search budget (Elsken et al. 2019). Excluding search does not make the remaining figures comparable either, because final runs also resist ranking by model size (table 10).

Elsken, Thomas, Jan Hendrik Metzen, and Frank Hutter. 2019. “Neural Architecture Search.” In The Springer Series on Challenges in Machine Learning, vol. 20. Springer International Publishing. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-05318-5_3.

The reported final run for the 600-billion-parameter GShard model emitted under a tenth as much as the 11-billion-parameter T5 run. One reason is architectural, because GShard-600B and the 1.5-trillion-parameter Switch Transformer are Mixture-of-Experts models that route each token to only a small subset of experts rather than activating every parameter on every token as dense T5, Meena, and GPT-3 do. Even so, the table records both lower energy use and lower emissions per megawatt-hour for GShard, and across the five runs the latter grid factor varies by a factor of about 3. Because the runs also differ in token budgets, accelerator generations, and data-center grids, the table’s totals do not isolate the saving from sparse activation alone. Parameter count therefore cannot rank their emissions, and operation count would still need an execution-efficiency and grid account. Search and failed experiments require a separate development inventory before these final-run figures can support a lifecycle comparison.

Addressing the design phase’s sustainability challenges requires innovations in training efficiency: sparse training, low-precision arithmetic, weight-sharing, and energy-aware NAS approaches. Transfer learning and fine-tuning pretrained models reuse pretrained representations instead of requiring every task to be trained from scratch (Raffel et al. 2020).

15.8.2 Water, chemicals, and critical materials

Before an accelerator exists, its fabrication consumes physical resources that an energy account alone does not quantify: ultrapure water, process chemicals, and materials. Their burdens occur across extraction, refining, fabrication, and assembly sites, rather than solely where the finished hardware operates. Water demand must be evaluated against local availability and competing uses, while material supply depends on the component and manufacturing process (Jha 2014; Chen 2006). These resource accounts extend the lifecycle boundary beyond carbon without reducing unlike impacts to a single energy total.

Jha, A. R. 2014. Rare Earth Materials: Properties and Applications. CRC Press. https://doi.org/10.1201/b17045.
Chen, H.-W. 2006. “Gallium, Indium, and Arsenic Pollution of Groundwater from a Semiconductor Manufacturing Area of Taiwan.” Bulletin of Environmental Contamination and Toxicology 77 (2): 289–96. https://doi.org/10.1007/s00128-006-1062-3.
Environmental Management—Water Footprint—Principles, Requirements and Guidelines, Pub. L. Nos. ISO 14046:2014 (2014).
The Green Grid. 2011. Water Usage Effectiveness (WUE): A Green Grid Data Center Sustainability Metric. White Paper 35. The Green Grid.

Water accounting requires more than a single WUE ratio. Water withdrawal measures water removed from a source, whereas water consumption measures the portion not returned to the same watershed in a usable form. The account must separate direct on-site cooling water, which equation 3 and the cooling-tower calculation in section 15.6.3 estimate, from indirect water used to generate electricity (figure 9) and manufacture hardware. The account must then attach each flow to its watershed, season, and local scarcity (Environmental Management—Water Footprint—Principles, Requirements and Guidelines 2014; The Green Grid 2011). One liter consumed during a wet season in a low-stress basin is not equivalent to one liter consumed during a drought in a stressed basin, so decision-grade comparisons report both physical volume and a stress-weighted result. Reclaimed-water percentages also need a declared denominator because recirculating water inside a facility can reduce withdrawal without eliminating evaporative consumption.

Semiconductor fabrication uses ultrapure water for chemical-mechanical planarization, wet etching, and wafer rinsing (Cooper et al. 2011).18 Complete environmental accounting requires auditing gross process flow, freshwater withdrawal, recycled volume, and evaporative consumption as distinct quantities, because internal reuse and flows that leave the facility change different totals (figure 14). Treatment returns reclaimed water to the intake stream and can reduce freshwater withdrawal, whereas evaporation remains a consumptive loss within the local water account. That classification does not mean the water permanently disappears from the hydrological cycle.

Cooper, Tom, Suzanne Fallender, Joyann Pafumi, Jon Dettling, Sebastien Humbert, and Lindsay Lessard. 2011. “A Semiconductor Company’s Examination of Its Water Footprint Approach.” Proceedings of the 2011 IEEE International Symposium on Sustainable Systems and Technology, 1–6. https://doi.org/10.1109/issst.2011.5936865.

18 Semiconductor water scale: A fab water inventory must separate intake, internal reuse, discharge, and consumption. Water per usable die depends on process flow, yield, allocation, and site conditions; a smaller process-node label does not establish a universal per-die multiplier.

The separate flows in figure 14 show why a recycling fraction cannot determine absolute consumption. The same reuse percentage can accompany very different intake volumes and evaporative losses. Facility siting therefore requires the underlying water volumes and local watershed conditions, not a reuse percentage alone.

Fabrication also uses chemical products for etching, doping, and cleaning: strong acids such as hydrofluoric and sulfuric acid, organic solvents, and toxic dopant gases such as arsine and phosphine. Their hazard comes from toxicity, reactivity, and the treatment burden of the waste streams they produce, not from their energy content. Chemical inventories and safety data sheets identify hazardous ingredients, although undisclosed formulations can limit the assessment (Kim et al. 2018). The environmental account needs the substances and quantities used at the specific facility, together with their release and treatment pathways. An energy total alone cannot describe these chemical burdens.

Kim, Sunju, Chungsik Yoon, Seunghon Ham, Jihoon Park, Ohun Kwon, Donguk Park, Sangjun Choi, Seungwon Kim, Kwonchul Ha, and Won Kim. 2018. “Chemical Use in the Semiconductor Manufacturing Industry.” International Journal of Occupational and Environmental Health 24 (3-4): 109–18. https://doi.org/10.1080/10773525.2018.1519957.
Rhodes, Christopher J. 2019. “Endangered Elements, Critical Raw Materials and Conflict Minerals.” Science Progress 102 (4): 304–50. https://doi.org/10.1177/0036850419884873.

AI hardware and its manufacturing supply chain depend on critical materials whose supply disruption can constrain production. Criticality is not synonymous with geological rarity or membership in the rare earth group. Materials such as indium appear in critical-materials and endangered-elements analyses because supply can depend on byproduct extraction, substitution options, and recycling constraints (Rhodes 2019). The relevant dependency may lie in a processor, package, peripheral component, or fabrication process (table 11).

The construction and operation of fabs and data centers also directly impact natural ecosystems through habitat disruption, water stress, and pollution from chemical discharge. Semiconductor-industry effluents can contaminate nearby fluvial sediments with heavy metals and trace elements (Hsu et al. 2016). Waste generation from fabrication, including gaseous emissions, VOC-laden air, and metal-contaminated wastewater, requires advanced treatment systems, and end-of-life hardware adds the e-waste stream examined in section 15.8.5. These water, chemical, and material demands are what fabrication spends to produce each usable accelerator, and the manufacturing phase that follows explains why an AI accelerator spends more of them than its wafer area suggests.

Hsu, Liang-Ching, Ching-Yi Huang, Yen-Hsun Chuang, Ho-Wen Chen, Ya-Ting Chan, Heng Yi Teah, Tsan-Yao Chen, Chiung-Fen Chang, Yu-Ting Liu, and Yu-Min Tzou. 2016. “Accumulation of Heavy Metals and Trace Elements in Fluvial Sediments Received Effluents from Traditional and Semiconductor Industries.” Scientific Reports 6 (1): 34250. https://doi.org/10.1038/srep34250.

15.8.3 Manufacturing phase

Fabricating an AI accelerator near the reticle limit, the maximum die area a single lithography exposure can print, leaves far fewer candidate dies per wafer than a small mobile processor, and every failed die transfers its share of ultrapure water, process chemicals, and lithography energy onto the working ones that survive. The manufacturing footprint combines the embodied carbon established in section 15.5.5 with the water, chemical, and material demands in section 15.8.2, and two structural properties of modern accelerators enlarge that resource investment per usable device.

First, because the probability that a die escapes every random wafer defect falls roughly exponentially with die area, a defect density that kills 5 percent of small dies may kill 20 percent or more of reticle-limit dies, concentrating the wafer’s fabrication burden onto a smaller surviving yield. Second, the memory bandwidth that large-scale training and inference require pushes accelerators toward advanced 2.5D and 3D packaging, such as TSMC’s Chip-on-Wafer-on-Substrate (CoWoS), which places high bandwidth memory stacks beside the logic die on an interposer. The inventory must include those memory stacks, the interposer, and the added assembly stages rather than counting only the accelerator die. Each usable accelerator therefore carries more process water, chemicals, and energy than its logic-die area alone suggests. Longer useful service spreads that manufacturing investment across more work, while lower-toxicity materials, recycling, and cleaner manufacturing address the production burden itself (Cenci et al. 2021; Irimia-Vladu 2014).

Cenci, Marcelo Pilotto, Tatiana Scarazzato, Daniel Dotto Munchen, Paula Cristina Dartora, Hugo Marcelo Veit, Andrea Moura Bernardes, and Pablo R. Dias. 2021. “Eco-Friendly Electronics—a Comprehensive Review.” Advanced Materials Technologies 7 (2): 2001263. https://doi.org/10.1002/admt.202001263.
Irimia-Vladu, M. 2014. “‘Green’ Electronics: Biodegradable and Biocompatible Materials and Devices for Sustainable Future.” Chemical Society Reviews 43 (2): 588–610. https://doi.org/10.1039/c3cs60235d.

15.8.4 Use phase

A GPT-3-scale training run draws 1,287 MWh over weeks of sustained cluster operation, and when those megawatt-hours are drawn matters just as much to the grid and the atmosphere as their sum. The operational energy consumed during training and inference is detailed in section 15.7; how flexible workloads shift that draw toward cleaner hours is developed in section 15.9.4. Once that active service ends, the remaining lifecycle question is what happens to the physical equipment itself.

15.8.5 Disposal, e-waste, and embedded AI

Data centers routinely retire working AI servers after three to four years, not because the silicon has physically failed, but because a newer generation’s capacity or efficiency leaves older racks uncompetitive for frontier workloads. Those hardware upgrade cycles (Slade 2007) connect AI deployment to the broader electronic waste (e-waste) problem. In 2019, global e-waste reached roughly 54 million metric tons (Forti et al. 2020), of which 17.4 percent was documented as formally collected and recycled (Singh and Ogunseitan 2022). These totals cover electrical and electronic equipment generally, not AI hardware alone. When equipment is finally discarded, hazardous substances in its components require controlled recovery and treatment rather than uncontrolled dumping or processing (Grossman 2007).

Slade, G. 2007. Made to Break: Technology and Obsolescence in America. Harvard University Press. https://doi.org/10.4159/9780674043756.
Forti, V., C. P. Baldé, R. Kuehr, and G. Bel. 2020. The Global e-Waste Monitor 2020: Quantities, Flows and the Circular Economy Potential. United Nations University/United Nations Institute for Training and Research; International Telecommunication Union; International Solid Waste Association.
Singh, Narendra, and Oladele A. Ogunseitan. 2022. “Disentangling the Worldwide Web of e-Waste and Climate Change Co-Benefits.” Circular Economy 1 (2): 100011. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cec.2022.100011.
Grossman, E. 2007. High Tech Trash: Digital Devices, Hidden Toxics, and Human Health. Island press.

Before hardware ever reaches that waste stream, memory capacity, bandwidth, and interconnect compatibility govern whether an older rack can be upgraded in place or must be retired as a unit. In tightly integrated systems, a new accelerator generation may require baseboard, networking, or server changes, whereas PCIe-based designs can offer modular upgrade options. Evaluating the actual compatibility boundary separates commercial retirement from physical disposal, because a functioning node retired from frontier training can still serve less demanding batch, development, or inference workloads.

The deployment of embedded AI integrates inference into edge appliances and consumer electronics. The broader connected-device fleet (figure 15) grew from an estimated 8.6 billion devices in 2019 to 15.1 billion in 2023, with 29.4 billion projected by 2030 (Statista 2022). These counts indicate the scale of the device ecosystem, not the number of AI systems or the amount of e-waste. Waste generation also depends on service life, repair, and reuse. Adhesive enclosures, nonreplaceable batteries, and proprietary components can make repair difficult, increasing the risk that a limited failure leads to whole-device disposal (Baldé et al. 2017).

Baldé, Cornelis P, Vanessa Forti, Vanessa Gray, Ruediger Kuehr, and Paul Stegmann. 2017. “The Global e-Waste Monitor 2017: Quantities, Flows and Resources.” United Nations University, International Telecommunication Union, International Solid Waste Association.

Limited software support, restricted repair, and difficult disassembly can push devices toward replacement instead of reuse. The lifecycle boundary must then follow the retired equipment to its next use or treatment destination. Moving hardware out of the operator’s inventory does not remove its environmental burden; the account must distinguish useful reuse, controlled recovery, and disposal that transfers costs to other communities.

15.8.6 Hardware lifespan extension

Keeping an AI server in useful service for 5 years instead of 3 years cuts the embodied carbon allocated to each year of operation by 40 percent.
