Countering the linear “take-make-dispose” model requires a shift toward a circular economy (Stahel 2016) that prioritizes reuse, refurbishment, and recycling, spreading a fixed manufacturing footprint over more service. This allocation result motivates evaluating longer use, but a claim of avoided emissions requires comparing the replacement and operating costs of the alternatives.

Stahel, Walter R. 2016. “The Circular Economy.” Nature 531 (7595): 435–38. https://doi.org/10.1038/531435a.
Federal Trade Commission. 2021. Nixing the Fix: An FTC Report to Congress on Repair Restrictions. Federal Trade Commission.

Four lifecycle interventions support longer hardware service by making systems repairable, upgradeable, supported, and reusable across their physical life. Physically, right-to-repair initiatives reduce service restrictions, including barriers to replacement parts, tools, and repair information (Federal Trade Commission 2021). Modular design separates replaceable subsystems so that an accelerator or network upgrade need not require discarding the entire chassis. Once the hardware remains serviceable, extended support cycles provide the driver updates and security patches needed to keep functioning equipment usable. When a node eventually falls behind frontier training demands, secondary-use programs match retired accelerators to workloads they can still serve, such as batch processing, development, or research. Each intervention must preserve the performance, compatibility, and security required for useful continued operation.

These interventions move sustainability from disposal management to lifecycle engineering. A replacement decision compares two ways of delivering the same future service, continued operation of the existing fleet and operation of a replacement fleet. The original manufacturing emissions have already occurred under either choice. Replacement adds a new manufacturing burden that pays back only through operational savings over the remaining service interval, so the tipping point is the time those savings take to equal it. Suppose a replacement accelerator carries the same allocated manufacturing footprint as an H100 (164 kg CO2e) and completes the same work with 50 percent less energy. Against an existing accelerator running continuously at the rated 700 W, the replacement repays its manufacturing emissions in about 6.5 weeks on the US-average grid. On Quebec’s grid it repays them only after about 2.7 years, nearly the whole of a 3-year standard service life. Lower utilization stretches both intervals in proportion. The same upgrade can therefore repay itself within weeks on a fossil-heavy grid and become a net loss on a clean one if the replacement leaves service before it breaks even. This accelerator-only account omits host and facility overhead, and disposal and any useful second life for the retired hardware shift it further, so a lower annual amortized figure alone cannot settle the decision (the financial side of the same refresh decision is screened in Refresh screening). Once an engineering team has measured both the operational and lifecycle terms of its workload account, the remaining question is how to combine algorithmic, infrastructure, and scheduling levers to drive those terms down under a real power or carbon ceiling.

Self-Check: Question

  1. For a fixed inventoried manufacturing footprint, what happens to annualized embodied carbon when useful hardware life extends from three years to five years, before considering changes in operational energy?

    1. The fixed manufacturing footprint is amortized over five years rather than three, reducing its annualized allocation by 40 percent; total lifecycle savings still depend on operational energy and the inventory boundary.
    2. Older accelerators automatically consume 40 percent less electrical power per FLOP as silicon pathways break in over time.
    3. Extending accelerator lifespans from three to five years eliminates electronic waste entirely because hardware decomposes organically after five years.
    4. Data center PUE automatically drops to 1.0 after servers have operated for more than four years.

  2. A research paper reports that training a new vision-language model consumed 500 MWh for its final successful training run. Explain why reporting only the final run systematically underestimates the development phase’s total carbon footprint, and identify two mitigation practices.

  3. True or False: If an AI training data center is powered 100 percent by dedicated geothermal and hydroelectric power, the use phase is completely unconstrained and engineers no longer need to consider grid dynamics or cooling efficiency.

  4. Standardized under ISO 14040 and ISO 14044, a comprehensive ____ evaluates the cumulative environmental impact of AI hardware across raw material extraction, manufacturing, use, and end-of-life disposal.

  5. A cloud provider considers retiring functional accelerators because a new generation offers a 12 percent improvement in performance per watt. Which option identifies a circular-economy candidate for evaluation, without claiming that this efficiency specification alone determines the lowest-emissions choice?

    1. Immediately shred and dispose of all prior-generation accelerators to eliminate any hardware with lower peak energy efficiency.
    2. Replace all servers instantly because any improvement in operational efficiency automatically offsets the embodied carbon of manufacturing a replacement fleet.
    3. Evaluate redeployment to compatible secondary workloads, comparing continued useful service with replacement manufacturing and measured operating emissions over the same remaining service interval.
    4. Overclock the older accelerators beyond thermal limits to match new hardware throughput regardless of cooling failure risk.

See Answers →

15.9 Mitigation Strategies

When a data center hits its absolute power ceiling, the operator cannot simply buy more GPUs. Supporting additional useful work requires reducing the power needed for that work, changing its schedule, or securing additional feasible capacity. Algorithmic optimization can reduce executed work and data movement; infrastructure changes can reduce overhead. Carbon-aware placement addresses a different constraint by changing the emissions associated with electricity use, even when energy demand stays fixed.

The measurement frameworks identify where environmental costs concentrate in a particular workload. Repeated experiments can make development dominant; sustained serving can make inference dominant; and clean electricity or limited use can increase the relative importance of manufacturing. The measured balance guides intervention. Algorithmic changes can reduce the number or cost of operations, infrastructure changes affect execution and facility overhead, and governance sets the budgets and incentives under which those choices operate.

Implementation must account for the rebound introduced with fleet growth in section 15.3, known as the Jevons paradox19 (principle 25). The tenfold-efficiency, hundredfold-volume example there supplied the arithmetic; the mechanism is that cheaper computation enables applications that were previously economically infeasible, so usage can grow enough to erase or exceed the expected energy savings. This rebound effect makes absolute outcomes, including carbon budgets, necessary companions to rate efficiency (FLOP/s per watt). Renewable sourcing changes the emissions associated with electricity but does not cap consumption. Governance must track workload growth against the intended resource budget to determine whether efficiency gains reduce the fleet’s total impact.

19 Jevons paradox: Jevons (1865) described how efficiency could stimulate demand enough to increase total coal use. Price reductions alone do not establish either energy factor; measure both unit energy and demand.

Jevons, William Stanley. 1865. The Coal Question: An Inquiry Concerning the Progress of the Nation, and the Probable Exhaustion of Our Coal Mines. Macmillan; Co.

15.9.1 Multi-layer mitigation

Compressing a model in half saves no carbon if the freed cluster capacity immediately fills with new experiments or if the job runs on a coal-heavy grid without a facility cap. Because a local efficiency gain can itself raise total consumption, mitigation must decide both which layer owns each reduction and which absolute budget contains the growth in demand that follows. Energy-efficient model design, optimized hardware deployment, sustainable infrastructure operations, and carbon-aware scheduling each attack a different term in the lifecycle footprint. Framework selection matters only insofar as it changes computation, memory movement, utilization, or reporting. Lifecycle-aware design keeps the optimization from ending at deployment by checking whether savings survive training, inference, manufacturing, and use.

Each mitigation lever acts at one accounting tier (figure 16). Work reduction and execution efficiency change IT energy. Facility overhead scales that energy to the facility total. A regional grid factor converts facility kilowatt-hours into operational carbon. Hardware amortization adds embodied emissions at its own boundary. Because these terms carry different units and compound at different layers, isolated efficiency percentages cannot be multiplied into a single composite reduction factor.

Rebound can cancel a reduction at any of these tiers. On an elastic demand curve (\(\DemandPriceElasticity> 1\)), a fall in unit cost raises volume enough to outweigh the saving (figure 17). When unit cost falls from \(\ComputeUnitCost_0\) to \(\ComputeUnitCost_1\), the green shaded area represents the saving on baseline volume \(\ComputeDemandVolume_0\). Demand expands to \(\ComputeDemandVolume_1\), creating a red area (\(\ComputeUnitCost_1 (\ComputeDemandVolume_1 - \ComputeDemandVolume_0)\)) that exceeds the original saving in this schematic. With monetary unit cost on the vertical axis, the areas measure expenditure. They represent energy only under an additional assumption that unit cost changes in proportion to energy per operation; an emissions comparison also needs the applicable carbon intensity.

Total energy depends on measured energy per query and query volume. Demand elasticity describes how volume responds to price; translating that response into energy requires a separate account of how unit energy changes.

Checkpoint 15.5: The efficiency trap (Jevons paradox)

An engineering team optimizes a translation service, reducing measured energy per query by 50 percent (2\(\times\) efficiency gain) at fixed quality. Assume the service passes that saving through as a proportional price reduction and that grid intensity stays unchanged.

The Jevons paradox does not invalidate efficiency as a strategy; it makes governance and capacity planning part of the result. At the level of individual systems, reducing energy per useful task remains a direct sustainability lever. A reduction in latency or monetary cost is a candidate improvement whose energy effect must still be measured.

Systems Perspective 15.4: Efficiency as sustainability
Model optimizations become sustainability tools only when they reduce measured lifecycle cost at fixed quality. Pruning can remove computation and memory traffic when the hardware skips the resulting structure. Quantization reduces representation size and may accelerate supported kernels, while knowledge distillation trades additional training for a smaller serving model. Each technique can also fail to reduce wall energy when its overhead or deployment regime leaves the dominant term unchanged.

Performance engineering supplies candidate interventions, but the environmental result still needs measurement. A faster run reduces energy only if the shorter duration outweighs any increase in average power; lower memory use helps only if it removes powered work or capacity. Hardware-software co-design becomes a sustainability gain when these savings survive the lifecycle boundary and the deployed workload volume.

Sustainable AI engineering overlaps strongly with efficient AI engineering but extends beyond it. The engineering principles that enable systems to scale, perform better, and cost less to operate can also reduce environmental impact, but sustainability adds lifecycle accounting, carbon-aware placement, absolute resource budgets, water and materials constraints, and governance against rebound effects. Sustainability is an integral part of good systems engineering, not a synonym for efficiency alone.

15.9.2 Lifecycle-aware development

Spending weeks quantizing a model whose footprint is dominated by repeated architecture searches, or moving a training job to a cleaner grid when year-round serving draws ten times more electricity, solves the wrong part of the inventory. Lifecycle-aware development starts from the largest environmental term in the workload and then selects the intervention that changes that term. Algorithmic design, infrastructure optimization, operational practice, and governance reduce impact only when they are sequenced around the measured bottleneck rather than applied as a generic checklist (Uddin and Rahman 2012).

Uddin, Mueen, and Azizah Abdul Rahman. 2012. “Energy Efficiency and Low Carbon Enabler Green IT Framework for Data Centers Considering Green Metrics.” Renewable and Sustainable Energy Reviews 16 (6): 4078–94. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.rser.2012.03.014.

Energy-efficient algorithmic design

Large deep learning models can require substantial computation during training and inference. The engineering question is how much of that work a particular task needs at its required quality. Parameter count alone does not identify redundant computation, and removing weights saves energy only if the resulting implementation avoids work or data movement. Sustainable algorithm design therefore evaluates the model and its execution together, measuring energy per useful task rather than treating a smaller representation as sufficient evidence of improvement.

When the measured bottleneck is unused structure, bit width, or serving scale, the sustainable design lever changes, because each bottleneck has its own intervention on the energy term (table 12).

The table’s three levers carry different deployment caveats. Pruning and quantization save energy only when the executed kernels skip arithmetic or move fewer bytes, which depends on sparsity structure, kernel support, and conversion overhead (section 15.4.1.2). Knowledge distillation20 instead amortizes its extra training cost across repeated serving.

20 Knowledge distillation: Introduced by Hinton et al. (2015), distillation trains a compact “student” model on soft probability targets from a larger “teacher,” capturing inter-class relationships that hard labels discard. The sustainability comparison must amortize teacher and student training across the actual serving volume and measure energy at the required output quality.

Hinton, Geoffrey, Oriol Vinyals, and Jeff Dean. 2015. “Distilling the Knowledge in a Neural Network.” arXiv Preprint.

Pruning, quantization, and distillation form the core toolkit for sustainable AI development, but their sustainability value depends on the measured bottleneck. The design question is not how to implement each compression method from first principles; it is how to rank these levers against memory movement, serving volume, carbon intensity, and lifecycle cost. A prioritized strategy orders them, together with carbon-aware scheduling, by the emissions term each one changes and attacks the largest term first.

Checkpoint 15.6: Prioritizing decarbonization strategy

Deploying a 70B LLM for a latency-sensitive application:

Architectures that avoid unneeded work

Architecture is a lever beyond compressing a given model, because it sets how much work the hardware performs at all. Specialized datapaths and local data reuse can reduce energy per useful operation, but a comparison must match workload, precision, achieved quality, and measurement boundary. Chip arithmetic alone excludes memory and supporting systems; end-to-end energy also includes their activity and execution time.

Under a fixed power envelope, the cheapest joule is the one the hardware never spends, so the choice of which work an architecture performs at all bears directly on the ceiling. Alternative computing architectures expose potential sources of efficiency: selective activation, local state, and reduced communication. Comparing their energy with a dense model requires a shared task, quality threshold, latency target, and measurement boundary.

The architectural opportunity is to avoid work that the task does not need. A periodically evaluated dense model may repeat computation on unchanged inputs, while an event-driven design can respond only when a relevant signal changes. The comparison reduces to two rates. A dense model evaluated \(\DenseEvaluationRate\) times per second at energy \(\DenseEvaluationEnergy\) per evaluation draws \(\DenseEvaluationRate \DenseEvaluationEnergy\) whether or not its input changed. An event-driven design that fires \(\InputEventRate\) times per second at energy \(\EventProcessingEnergy\) draws \(\InputEventRate \EventProcessingEnergy+ \EventDetectionPower\), where \(\EventDetectionPower\) is the constant power of the circuitry that watches for events. Skipping work pays only while \(\InputEventRate \EventProcessingEnergy+ \EventDetectionPower< \DenseEvaluationRate \DenseEvaluationEnergy\), so the advantage shrinks as inputs change more often or as detection itself grows expensive. Dense execution can also reuse data and power down idle circuitry, so the comparison must examine the implemented workload rather than the architecture label alone.

Powering down inactive components helps either design only where the hardware supports it, and static power, sensing, and communication overhead remain part of the energy budget. Local learning and improved sample efficiency address different costs. Keeping an update local can reduce communication, while requiring fewer examples or optimization steps can reduce training work; neither property guarantees the other. Evaluation must include auxiliary preprocessing, adaptation, and inference costs at the required accuracy. Sparsity and sparse interconnects offer another route when execution skips arithmetic or transfers, but indexing, routing, load imbalance, and synchronization can consume the nominal saving.

Event-driven and sparse computation point toward research directions for sustainable AI. Architectures that implement spiking neural networks (SNNs), event-driven models that communicate through discrete spikes, or sparse activation patterns can reduce energy. A neuron that does not spike neither computes nor sends a message, so switching and communication track input activity rather than a fixed clock21 (Prakash et al. 2023).

21 Spiking neural networks (SNNs): Sparse spiking activity can reduce communication and switching, but the complete comparison includes training, encoding, sensing, and the hardware used to execute the model. A saving measured on one temporal task does not transfer automatically to other workloads.

Prakash, Shvetank, Matthew Stewart, Colby Banbury, Mark Mazumder, Pete Warden, Brian Plancher, and Vijay Janapa Reddi. 2023. “Is TinyML Sustainable? Assessing the Environmental Impacts of Machine Learning on Microcontrollers.” ArXiv Preprint abs/2301.11899.

These directions expand the design space without making one architecture universally sustainable. The relevant improvement is less measured lifecycle impact per useful task at the required quality and service level. Dense, sparse, and event-driven implementations must meet the same comparison contract.

Lifecycle-aware systems

Repeated model updates add to the lifecycle footprint even when each deployed version is efficient. The relevant comparison is the work required to maintain the service’s quality over time, including development and retraining. A retraining cycle that repeats the development sweep of section 15.5.2 pays its 5× final-run multiple again on every update. Transfer learning and fine-tuning can reuse a pretrained model instead of repeating training from scratch (Raffel et al. 2020), but the comparison must include adaptation, evaluation, and any additional work needed to recover quality on the target task. Reuse is a candidate saving whose value depends on that complete update cycle.

Raffel, C., N. Shazeer, A. Roberts, K. Lee, S. Narang, M. Matena, Y. Zhou, W. Li, and P. J. Liu. 2020. “Exploring the Limits of Transfer Learning with a Unified Text-to-Text Transformer.” Journal of Machine Learning Research 21 (140): 1–67.
Xu, Dianlei, Tong Li, Yong Li, Xiang Su, Sasu Tarkoma, Tao Jiang, Jon Crowcroft, and Pan Hui. 2020. “Edge Intelligence: Architectures, Challenges, and Applications.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2003.12172.

The deployment boundary matters just as much as the training boundary. Edge deployment can reduce communication energy by running inference on specialized low-power hardware at the point of use (Xu et al. 2020), but the lifecycle account must include the embodied carbon of manufacturing and replacing those devices. Embedding LCA methodologies into AI workflows allows teams to see this trade-off early: a cloud model, an edge model, and a hybrid cascade may have different winners depending on query volume, device lifetime, grid carbon intensity, and retraining frequency (International Organization for Standardization 2006a, 2006b). Henderson et al. (2020) argue for systematic reporting of ML energy and carbon footprints precisely so these comparisons can be made consistently. As the Jevons paradox warns, the accounting must also include usage growth, because optimizing one stage may increase total impact if lower costs enable wider deployment.

Benchmarks and operating metrics

Benchmarks matter when they make efficiency visible at procurement and design time. The ML.ENERGY Leaderboard (ML.ENERGY Initiative et al. 2023) reports inference energy measurements under declared model and serving configurations. MLPerf power submissions pair performance results with system-level power measurements under benchmark rules. These results support comparisons within the stated workload, quality, and measurement conditions; converting them into carbon footprints still requires electricity and lifecycle accounting beyond the benchmark.

ML.ENERGY Initiative, Jae-Won Chung, Jiachen Gu, Zhewei Yan, Tony Lo, Yejin Park, and Mosharaf Chowdhury. 2023. The ML.ENERGY Leaderboard. SymbioticLab, University of Michigan.

The right efficiency metric depends on the serving regime. Batch inference is naturally expressed as correct samples per joule, latency-sensitive serving as queries per joule that meet the service-level objective (SLO), and generative workloads as joules per accepted output token at a declared quality threshold. Each is fleet energy productivity (equation 1.5) with useful work counted in the regime’s own unit, and those queries are the requests that SLO goodput credits (The inversion from training to inference). Raw throughput per joule rewards systems that lower accuracy, truncate outputs, or miss latency objectives. Standardization does not make one platform universally green; it fixes useful work, quality, the measurement window, and the energy denominator well enough for procurement and architecture decisions to be argued quantitatively.

For sub-watt TinyML deployments, the MLPerf Tiny benchmark suite applies this discipline to constrained devices (Banbury et al. 2021). Tasks include visual wake words, keyword spotting, anomaly detection, and image classification. The closed division constrains implementations to reference-equivalent models, while the open division permits broader model choices. Reading accuracy, latency, and energy together within the declared division prevents a change in task or model quality from masquerading as a hardware-efficiency gain.

Banbury, Colby, Vijay Janapa Reddi, Peter Torelli, Jeremy Holleman, Nat Jeffries, Csaba Kiraly, Pietro Montino, et al. 2021. “MLPerf Tiny Benchmark.” arXiv Preprint.

Energy and latency still have to be read together through the energy-delay product (EDP) (equation 16), which balances energy consumption against response time by penalizing solutions that save power only by taking too long:

\[\text{EDP} = \TotalEnergy \times \Time= \AveragePower\times \Time^2 \tag{16}\]

where \(\TotalEnergy\) is energy consumed, \(\Time\) is execution time, and \(\AveragePower\) is average power. The quadratic delay term penalizes solutions that achieve low energy through excessive delays. Lower EDP is preferable under this particular trade-off score, after quality and latency requirements have been satisfied. It does not authorize missing a service deadline or replace energy per completed task.

For TinyML deployments, EDP provides a score for comparing feasible operating points. Reducing clock frequency can lower power while extending execution; reducing voltage can lower switching energy as well, when the hardware permits it. Static power and the longer active interval can offset those gains. The preferred setting therefore comes from measured energy and delay across supported configurations, after excluding settings that miss the workload’s deadlines.

Sustainability metrics complement performance benchmarks by recording the resource cost of useful work. Regulation is beginning to require such records. The EU AI Act’s documentation obligations for covered general-purpose models, examined with the other regulatory mechanisms in section 15.10.1, include known or estimated energy consumption. Such requirements make the measurement boundary and provenance part of the reporting evidence rather than an optional annotation to a performance result.

15.9.3 Infrastructure optimization

Even the best-tuned training kernel cannot control how much power the chillers and transformers spend around the rack, or how much coal the regional grid burns to supply the substation. While algorithmic optimizations reduce operation counts, data movement, or the energy of execution, the surrounding operational environment determines the facility overhead and emissions associated with that work. Renewable energy integration, carbon-aware workload scheduling, and cooling optimization act on these surrounding terms, so each intervention must be evaluated at the boundary it changes.

A single hyperscale data center can consume over 100 MW of power, comparable to a small city.22 Renewable energy integration, advanced cooling, and AI-driven optimization offer complementary interventions. Their value depends on whether the measured problem lies in the electricity source, heat-removal overhead, or operating controls.

22 PUE gap: The PUE model in section 15.4.1.3 shows that facility overhead scales with every watt of IT load. At 100 MW, even a small PUE change therefore represents megawatts of cooling and power-delivery capacity, but its carbon value still depends on the grid signal.

Power availability and power source are connected infrastructure decisions, but annual energy matching does not establish continuous supply. Organizations training large models may locate near renewable sources or purchase renewable energy certificates (RECs) to claim the attributes of renewable generation. A certificate does not physically deliver electricity or remove the facility’s demand from the grid; the design must still provide power when and where the workload runs.

Procurement can also support additional low-carbon supply. Microsoft’s 2024 agreement with Constellation supported a planned nuclear-reactor restart, while Google’s geothermal partnership with Fervo began supplying the Nevada grid in 2023. These examples address the need for generation beyond variable wind and solar output. They do not make one generation technology mandatory; the engineering requirement is a supply portfolio that meets the facility’s demand across time, with transmission, storage, and workload flexibility accounted for.

Major cloud providers have announced renewable-energy commitments, but variable generation leaves a supply-matching problem. Storage and flexible scheduling can help address it alongside transmission and other generation sources; the appropriate combination depends on the site and workload. Google’s published 2030 target for 24/7 carbon-free energy23 illustrates the harder version of this goal: matching electricity consumption with carbon-free generation, including nuclear as well as renewables, hour by hour rather than only on an annual basis.

23 24/7 carbon-free energy (CFE): Hourly matching strengthens the time resolution of electricity procurement and accounting. Its physical impact depends on deliverability, additional clean supply, storage, and grid conditions. It does not eliminate manufacturing emissions or prove that every consumed electron came from a carbon-free generator.

Cooling overhead depends on rack density, climate, and the heat-removal design, as quantified in section 15.6.3. Liquid cooling can support dense accelerator configurations, but its energy benefit depends on the complete pumping and heat-rejection system. Control policy supplies another lever. The DeepMind case in section 15.4.1.4 shows how measured operating data can guide cooling adjustments within thermal constraints; its reported savings describe the evaluated site rather than a universal reduction for any facility.

15.9.4 Carbon-aware placement and scheduling

A batch training job that consumes the exact same megawatt-hours can emit several times more carbon if it runs during an evening fossil-gas peak or in a coal-heavy region than if it waits for a windy afternoon or runs on a hydro-heavy grid. Placement and scheduling change the emissions attributed to a fixed workload rather than the energy it uses: placement chooses the region a job runs in, and scheduling chooses its hours. Both depend on the grid signal they act on and on the additional energy the move itself causes.

↩︎ Scheduling against an external price signal, trading waiting and preemption against deadlines, is established in Cost-aware scheduling policies.

Regional grid intensity data sources

Every carbon-aware decision inherits the quality of the grid signal behind it, and the right signal depends on the decision. Grid carbon intensity varies with generation mix, which changes hourly with demand, renewable availability, and plant dispatch. The US Energy Information Administration publishes historical grid emissions factors by region, updated annually. These averages give a coarse baseline for inventories or early planning, but they cannot resolve the hourly effect of moving a flexible workload. Electricity Maps provides time-resolved, flow-traced average and lifecycle intensity estimates across covered grids, while WattTime provides marginal operating-emissions signals that estimate the generator response to added or removed load.

The two kinds of signal answer the two questions that the measurement contract (section 15.4) separated. Time-matched average factors support the attributional inventory of a completed run. Marginal factors support the consequential question of how shifting or eliminating a run would change system emissions, which is the question a scheduler asks.

Placement across regions and hours

Carbon intensity varies across geographic locations and time periods, creating optimization opportunities. Temporal scheduling can reduce emissions when deadline-tolerant workloads are shifted toward lower-carbon hours or regions, with the realized gain depending on workload flexibility, grid mix, and whether the scheduler uses average or marginal emissions (Patterson et al. 2022; Radovanovic et al. 2021). Carbon-aware scheduling automates that shift, moving flexible, nonurgent work toward times and regions with lower carbon intensity while respecting its deadline and the available capacity.

A real placement decision weighs more than the grid factor. Adding electricity prices, facility overhead, and an internal carbon price turns the comparison into a placement objective, priced as a modeled total cost of ownership (TCO) over three candidate regions (the US-average grid, Quebec, and Iowa). Notebook 15.2 isolated the grid factor by holding facility energy fixed. This objective must instead let facility overhead differ from region to region, so it starts one boundary earlier, holding the run’s IT energy fixed and letting each region apply its own PUE. That choice separates the carbon charge from the cost of facility overhead.

↩︎ The total-cost-of-ownership model that prices ownership, maintenance, and electricity is established in The TCO equation.

Example 15.1: Automated carbon-aware placement
Scenario: A team is planning a large training run requiring 10,000 MWh of IT energy, which occupies an 8,192-GPU frontier-scale cluster for about 112 days. The team is choosing among three candidate regions (US average, Quebec, and Iowa) with different facility overheads (PUE) and grid carbon intensities.

Diagnosis: With an internal carbon tax of $100/tonne, the placement model adds a carbon charge to each region’s modeled cost of ownership and selects Quebec for a total cost of $25.3M. Hardware ownership and maintenance make up most of that total and are the same in every region, so the candidates differ only in facility overhead and carbon. Without a carbon price, Quebec’s lower PUE saves about $72,000 against the US-average placement. With the tax, the US-average and Iowa placements cost $0.5M and $0.8M more than Quebec, and most of each gap is the carbon charge.

Systems lesson: Carbon-aware placement adds an explicit carbon price to the fleet scheduling objective. The selected region minimizes the modeled cost among these candidates under the stated assumptions. A deployment decision must also check capacity, data transfer, deadlines, and reliability.

The placement example compares regions using a fixed annual grid factor for each candidate. Temporal scheduling adds another decision by allowing a flexible job to wait for lower-carbon hours within one region. These are distinct levers, and their relative value depends on the available regions and the job’s deadline; section 15.9.4.3 develops the temporal lever with the marginal signal that the measurement contract assigns to it.

Time-shifting under deadlines

Carbon-aware scheduling is a load-shifting software problem. The scheduler queries one of the grid signals compared above and combines it with workload flexibility. A feasible shift must finish before the deadline, retain access to data and accelerator capacity, and preserve the job’s reliability constraints. Its net benefit must exceed checkpoint, transfer, restart, and idle-capacity emissions. Pausing useful work without reducing the cluster’s powered load can erase the expected saving.

Google’s carbon-intelligent computing platform illustrates this approach by using forecasts and virtual capacity limits to delay flexible work (Radovanovic et al. 2021). In the multi-tier accounting cascade of figure 16, temporal load shifting modulates the grid emissions factor applied to facility electricity rather than reducing raw compute FLOPs. That is a carbon intervention, not automatically an energy reduction; checkpointing, transfer, and a different runtime can also change electricity consumption.

Radovanovic, Ana, Ross Koningstein, Ian Schneider, Bokan Chen, Alexandre Duarte, Binz Roy, Diyue Xiao, et al. 2021. “Carbon-Aware Computing for Datacenters.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2106.11750, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2106.11750.

Because a scheduler asks how moving a job changes emissions, it needs a marginal signal rather than the average factor an inventory uses, and the providers compared above expose signals with different methodologies and coverage. A feasible schedule must satisfy three constraints on one time axis at once, the grid carbon signal \(\text{CO}_{2,\text{grid}}(\TimeInstant)\), the job’s execution state, and cluster headroom \(\ClusterHeadroom(\TimeInstant)\) (figure 18). The job waits, checkpointed, through the high-carbon peak, runs for \(\JobDuration\) in a low-carbon trough where headroom exists, and still finishes with slack before its deadline. Waiting pays only when the avoided grid emissions exceed the checkpoint I/O, idle standby, and state migration that the wait itself causes.

A large distributed training run draws sustained aggregate power with the correlated phase transitions described in section 15.6.2, but its completion deadline may leave scheduling flexibility. Checkpoints created for fault recovery also provide the restart points for the deliberate pause in figure 18. The decision requires a local emissions forecast and enough deadline slack; late-afternoon or overnight operation is not universally preferable. Fault tolerance thus supplies a mechanism for flexibility without proving that every pause saves emissions. Online inference has tighter delay budgets and may support bounded preemption, while offline inference can often be deferred more freely.

↩︎ Checkpoint-restart mechanics and the failure-driven choice of checkpoint interval are established in Checkpointing and Progress Preservation.

The duck curve describes the steep evening ramp in net load (total demand minus variable solar and wind output) as midday solar generation falls toward sunset while residential and commercial demand rises. Because grid operators typically meet that rapid net-load climb by dispatching fast-ramping fossil-gas generators, the evening ramp often coincides with a sharp spike in marginal carbon intensity. A constant data-center load raises the baseline level of net demand but does not by itself increase the evening ramp rate. A flexible training load, by contrast, can reduce both the required ramp and its own emissions by curtailing during that interval. Schedulers should still act on local forecasts and marginal emissions signals rather than a fixed daily pause window, which would ignore regional generation and transmission conditions.

Renewable generation variability complicates batch training dispatch across power grids. Over one week of November 2023, the European Union’s hourly generation mix moved across days as well as within each day (figure 19). Demand peaked each day between 326 GW and 367 GW. Nuclear held near 73 GW, solar contributed midday output, and wind ranged from 27 GW on Sunday to 111 GW on Thursday. Wind contributes to that variation, but demand, other generation, storage, and electricity exchanges also affect fossil dispatch. Carbon-aware scheduling therefore needs regional grid forecasts rather than a fixed assumption about which hours are cleanest.

15.9.5 Google sustainability framework case study

When Google engineers compared a baseline Transformer training setup against one that combined a sparser architecture, custom TPUs, an efficient cloud facility, and a low-carbon grid, they reported an 83-fold reduction in energy and a 747-fold reduction in operational \(\text{CO}_2\) emissions (Patterson et al. 2022). The value of Google’s case study is that it groups mitigation into four levers that correspond to terms this chapter has tracked, namely the model’s work, the machine’s efficiency, the facility’s overhead, and the grid’s carbon factor. Google engineers identified these “4 Ms” (table 13) for reducing the carbon footprint of rapidly expanding AI workloads (Patterson et al. 2022).

In Patterson et al. (2022), that 83-fold energy reduction and 747-fold CO2 reduction come from a consistent accounting model supported by measurements across compatible workloads and boundaries; multiplying unrelated best-case component ratios would not establish the same result. In the period studied, AI workloads also represented less than 15 percent of Google’s total energy consumption despite expanding deployment. That share is evidence about relative consumption. It does not by itself establish constant or declining absolute energy when the denominator also changes, so engineers must assess absolute growth from measured energy totals alongside the reported efficiency improvements.

Patterson, David, Joseph Gonzalez, Urs Holzle, Quoc Le, Chen Liang, Lluis-Miquel Munguia, Daniel Rothchild, David R. So, Maud Texier, and Jeff Dean. 2022. “The Carbon Footprint of Machine Learning Training Will Plateau, Then Shrink.” Computer 55 (7): 18–28. https://doi.org/10.1109/mc.2022.3148714.
Du, Nan, Yanping Huang, Andrew M. Dai, Simon Tong, Dmitry Lepikhin, Yuanzhong Xu, Maxim Krikun, et al. 2022. “GLaM: Efficient Scaling of Language Models with Mixture-of-Experts.” In International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), edited by Kamalika Chaudhuri, Stefanie Jegelka, Le Song, Csaba Szepesvari, Gang Niu, and Sivan Sabato, vol. 162. Proceedings of Machine Learning Research. PMLR.

Empirical case studies demonstrate how sustainable-AI engineering can improve both capability and environmental impact. For example, Patterson et al. (2022) compare GPT-3 with Google’s GLaM and report improved quality metrics alongside reduced training computation and lower-carbon energy sources, while the GLaM model paper explains the mixture-of-experts architecture behind that efficient scaling (Du et al. 2022).

↩︎ Mixture-of-experts execution and the AllToAll routing it requires are established in Expert parallelism (mixture of experts).

The strategy in the case study, which combines systematic measurement, carbon-aware development, transparency in reporting, and renewable energy transition, provides a practical template for sustainable AI scaling. The analysis also shows that earlier extrapolations overstated ML energy requirements by large factors when they ignored empirical efficiency gains and measurement boundaries. Technical levers alone, however, only save carbon when organizations have an incentive to deploy them and a bound that prevents rebound growth, which brings the engineering account to external policy, regulation, and governance.

Self-Check: Question

  1. An AI company deploys quantization and knowledge distillation, reducing the energy cost per user query by 50 percent. Over the following year, the company’s total data center electricity consumption increases by 75 percent as lower per-query costs enable new product features and attract 3.5\(\times\) more queries. Which phenomenon explains this outcome?

    1. Amdahl’s law, which states that sequential execution limits parallel speedup.
    2. Dennard scaling breakdown, where transistor power density increases with scaling.
    3. Jevons paradox, where technological efficiency improvements lower the effective cost of a resource, stimulating increased demand that outweighs per-unit efficiency gains.
    4. The memory wall, where DRAM bandwidth fails to keep pace with arithmetic throughput.

  2. A model compression team evaluates unstructured weight pruning, INT8 quantization, and knowledge distillation for serving a transformer. Explain the hardware mechanisms that can turn each technique into measured energy savings, and why theoretical compression alone is insufficient.

  3. A systems team aims to overcome the energy wall for future foundation model architectures. Which design direction most directly targets the physical root cause of energy waste identified in the chapter?

    1. Increasing clock frequency and supply voltage to maximize compute throughput per unit silicon area.
    2. Enforcing dense, synchronized execution across all model layers so accelerator execution units maintain 100 percent active duty cycles.
    3. Adopting event-driven and sparse-activation architectures that execute compute only on salient tokens or state changes, minimizing dense data movement across memory hierarchies.
    4. Replacing FP16 tensor cores with FP32 standard execution units to maximize numeric accuracy per memory access.

  4. A platform team has recurring batch training jobs that can move across regions or time while preserving quality. Which intervention is a candidate for lowering operational carbon without changing the model, provided capacity, data access, deadlines, and net emissions are checked?

    1. Replacing all ReLU activation functions with GELU activations across training scripts.
    2. Increasing the batch size to maximize GPU execution unit occupancy.
    3. Switching from PyTorch to a C++ inference engine for training loops.
    4. Implementing carbon-aware spatial and temporal scheduling to route training jobs to data center regions and time windows with surplus renewable energy and low carbon intensity.

  5. Explain the difference between average and marginal grid emissions, and identify the evidence a scheduler needs to estimate the emissions effect of a proposed workload shift.

  6. Order the following stages of a carbon-aware scheduler’s decision: (1) compare complete projected emissions of feasible candidates, including checkpoint, transfer, restart, and idle costs, (2) obtain documented grid forecasts and workload requirements, (3) dispatch the selected feasible action, (4) monitor progress and re-evaluate against constraints, (5) filter candidates by deadline, capacity, data access, power, cooling, and reliability requirements.

See Answers →

15.10 Policy, Regulation, and the Path Forward

Electricity prices reward some efficiency gains, but they need not capture water stress, manufacturing emissions, or local environmental burdens. A training cluster can reduce its electricity bill by moving to a cheaper region while increasing its emissions. If the bill omits that environmental cost, a cost-minimizing placement decision may favor the higher-emissions site. Reporting requirements expose the trade-off; carbon pricing, procurement conditions, and other policy instruments can change the incentives. Their effect depends on which activities they cover and how organizations implement them.

For a systems reader, policy is the control plane that changes the objective function. Reporting rules make hidden energy and embodied-carbon costs measurable, carbon pricing turns location into a scheduling variable, and procurement standards make lifecycle accounting part of infrastructure design. The regulatory mechanisms in section 15.10.1 matter because they determine which optimizations become economically rational at fleet scale.

15.10.1 Regulatory mechanisms

An organization training a frontier model above \(10^{25}\) FLOPs or operating a large European subsidiary can face legal requirements to disclose energy consumption and value-chain emissions, yet neither law requires metering every individual AI job. Reporting rules act at different organizational boundaries: the EU AI Act24 includes model-level technical-documentation obligations, while the Corporate Sustainability Reporting Directive (CSRD)25 addresses sustainability reporting by covered companies. Model energy records can inform both, so engineers must identify the covered entity or model, applicable dates and exemptions, and the evidence the reporting method requires.

24 EU AI Act (2024): The Act presumes high-impact capabilities above \(10^{25}\) FLOPs of training compute and imposes additional obligations on general-purpose models with systemic risk. Annex XI includes computational resources and known or estimated energy consumption in required technical documentation; qualifying open-source models without systemic risk can be exempt from these documentation obligations. Article 101 permits fines for specified provider violations up to 3 percent of annual worldwide turnover or EUR 15 million, whichever is higher. Application dates, model scope, and exemptions must be checked before turning these provisions into an engineering reporting requirement.

25 Corporate Sustainability Reporting Directive (CSRD): Directive (EU) 2026/470 narrows reporting thresholds to more than 1,000 employees and more than EUR 450 million in net annual turnover, assessed at group level where applicable. Member States must transpose its reporting amendments by March 19, 2027; entity scope, exemptions, and reporting-year rules still determine applicability. Covered reporting follows European Sustainability Reporting Standards rather than a separate inventory of AI jobs. The engineering contribution is traceable information on relevant electricity, procurement, and value-chain impacts.

26 Emissions trading for compute: Applying cap-and-trade to AI emissions is a policy analogy here, not a description of an established compute-allowance market. Such a design would need a declared emissions boundary, measurement method, enforcement mechanism, and rules for trading allowances.

Policy can also impose a priced resource constraint. A hypothetical emissions cap for AI operations would specify tonnes of CO2e and allow trading of unused emissions allowances, following the general logic of emissions trading systems (ETS).26 A power cap in MW or an energy budget in MWh would constrain different quantities and must not be called the same credit. The EU Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism, by contrast, applies to embedded emissions in specified imported goods and electricity; it does not generally price an AI service by its compute location.

Incentives and procurement conditions can change which designs organizations choose. A grant can support efficiency research, while a procurement contract can require declared energy measurements or lifecycle evidence. These conditions need measurable criteria rather than an undefined “green” label. Scope 3 emissions also depend on whose inventory is being prepared: purchased hardware and outsourced cloud services enter the customer’s value-chain account, while the supplier classifies its own activities within its organizational boundary. The same physical emissions must not be added twice merely because two organizations report them.

15.10.2 Industry self-regulation and standards

A cloud provider can announce “100 percent annual renewable matching” while its data centers still draw fossil-generated electricity every night when solar output drops to zero. Voluntary commitments and common standards supplement government requirements, but an announced target is not evidence of a measured reduction. Annual renewable-energy matching and 24/7 carbon-free energy (CFE) address different time resolutions: hourly matching makes periods of unmatched demand visible, while the resulting physical emissions still depend on the generation and grid context (Monyei and Jenkins 2018). Evaluating a provider’s progress therefore requires the reported method and results, rather than merely its announced target.

Monyei, C. G., and Kirsten E. H. Jenkins. 2018. “Electrons Have No Identity: Setting Right Misrepresentations in Google and Apple’s Clean Energy Purchasing.” Energy Research & Social Science 46: 48–51. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.erss.2018.06.015.
Schmidt, Victor, Kamal Goyal, Aditya Joshi, Boris Feld, Liam Conell, Nikolas Laskaris, Doug Blank, Jonathan Wilson, Sorelle Friedler, and Sasha Luccioni. 2021. CodeCarbon: Estimate and Track Carbon Emissions from Machine Learning Computing. Zenodo software citation. https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.4658424.
Lacoste, Alexandre, Alexandra Luccioni, Victor Schmidt, and Thomas Dandres. 2019. “Quantifying the Carbon Emissions of Machine Learning.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1910.09700.

Internal carbon pricing gives emissions a weight in project decisions. A “shadow price” lets an organization compare alternatives using both financial cost and attributed emissions; its influence depends on the price, accounting boundary, and whether decision makers use it. Measurement tools supply inputs to that comparison. Projects like CodeCarbon and ML CO2 Impact let developers estimate model carbon footprints within their workflows (Schmidt et al. 2021; Lacoste et al. 2019), but the estimates still need declared power, location, and allocation assumptions.

Standardized benchmarks provide evidence for particular efficiency claims. MLCommons defines MLPerf power-measurement protocols that connect system energy with workload performance under declared conditions. The Green Software Foundation’s Software Carbon Intensity specification addresses a different boundary, combining operational and allocated embodied emissions per functional unit. A benchmark result or a calculation method is not itself an independent sustainability audit. Accountability requires checking the reported inputs, scope, and comparison against the claim being made.

15.10.3 Public engagement and transparency

A sustainability report that trumpets a 50 percent drop in energy per query while omitting a fourfold expansion in total cluster capacity, or that leaves hardware manufacturing out of its inventory, masks a growing footprint behind a selective boundary. Misleading presentation of environmental performance, or greenwashing,27 can arise from selective boundaries or omitted trade-offs as well as unsupported offset claims. Effective AI sustainability governance requires evidence that matches the claim, combining workload telemetry with facility measurements and supply-chain inventories. Runtime power counters alone cannot establish Scope 1–3 completeness or the environmental costs of manufacturing.

27 Greenwashing in AI: Typical forms are claiming “carbon neutrality” through offsets while expanding data center capacity, or highlighting per-query efficiency gains while total compute grows faster. Green-claims rules can require verifiable evidence for environmental claims. For ML engineers, the technical litmus test is whether sustainability reporting covers all three GHG Protocol scopes, or conveniently omits Scope 3 (hardware manufacturing, cloud supply chain) where much of AI’s carbon can reside.

Pledge-style disclosure is useful only when it creates auditable data rather than reputational cover. The Montréal Carbon Pledge, originally a commitment by institutional investors to measure and disclose carbon footprints annually, is a useful model precisely because its value lies in the disclosed data, not the pledge itself. The same standard applies to an AI organization, whose sustainability claims are only as credible as the workload-level measurements behind them.

15.10.4 Environmental justice and responsible development

When a hyperscale data center is sited in a drought-prone agricultural basin or beside a neighborhood served by peaking gas turbines, local residents absorb the water stress and air pollution while the model’s outputs serve users thousands of miles away. Transparency establishes the evidence base for those impacts; environmental justice asks how the burdens and benefits revealed by that evidence are distributed. Site selection is where much of that distribution is decided, which makes it an environmental-justice decision rather than a facilities decision alone. Environmental sustainability extends ML systems responsibility from model behavior to ecological stewardship (Vinuesa et al. 2020). The computational resources required for AI development concentrate environmental costs on specific communities while distributing benefits unequally across global populations. Data centers impose both grid demand and cooling-water demand, and the local burden depends on the energy mix, watershed, and competing regional uses.

Vinuesa, Ricardo, Hossein Azizpour, Iolanda Leite, Madeline Balaam, Virginia Dignum, Sami Domisch, Anna Felländer, Simone Daniela Langhans, Max Tegmark, and Francesco Fuso Nerini. 2020. “The Role of Artificial Intelligence in Achieving the Sustainable Development Goals.” Nature Communications 11 (1): 233. https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-019-14108-y.

28 Environmental justice in data center siting: A site-cost model can omit burdens borne by nearby residents, such as competing water demand or changes to local infrastructure. A low financial cost therefore does not establish a low community burden. Site evaluation must identify who benefits, who bears the resource costs, and how affected communities participate in the decision alongside the analysis of grid intensity and water stress.

That geographic concentration creates environmental-justice risks that align with broader responsible AI frameworks.28 The fairness claim here is narrow and systems-specific. An ML fleet allocates benefits, electricity demand, water use, land pressure, and e-waste across different communities. Communities hosting AI infrastructure can bear disproportionate environmental burdens while having limited access to AI’s economic benefits.

These distributional concerns fall into four groups (figure 20). Environmental justice concerns local water withdrawal and grid strain. Intergenerational equity concerns atmospheric \(\text{CO}_2\) persistence and long-lived infrastructure. Resource competition concerns agricultural and municipal claims on land, water, and minerals. The transparency gap concerns undisclosed per-model energy, carbon, and water records. Engineering acts most directly on the last group, because auditable telemetry is what allows the other three to be assessed at all; the measurement contract of section 15.4 supplies that telemetry.

Impact assessments and community participation make these trade-offs visible before a site is committed. Any claim that such an assessment is legally required must identify the applicable jurisdiction, rule, and project scope. Where siting and governance bound the burden of current facilities, longer-term research targets the architectural and measurement limits that make AI workloads so resource-intensive in the first place.

15.10.5 Future research directions

Even after a team applies every scheduling, cooling, and compression lever in this chapter, conventional accelerators still spend most of their dynamic energy shuttling weights and activations back and forth across memory buses, while most teams still lack real-time, per-job carbon telemetry. The research agenda therefore follows the same engineering logic as the mitigation framework: reduce data movement, close the measurement gap, and avoid redundant computation. Non-von Neumann computing architectures,29 including neuromorphic computing and in-memory computing, can reduce selected transfers, extending the event-driven and spiking designs of section 15.9.2.2 from avoided work to avoided movement. Evaluate the remaining arithmetic, conversion, control, and peripheral energy at the required quality; eliminating one cost does not eliminate the whole system account.

29 Von Neumann bottleneck: Separating processing from memory requires data transfers whose energy depends on locality and arithmetic intensity. If movement contributes a fraction \(\DataMovementEnergyFraction\) of total energy, eliminating that term alone gives at most a \(1/(1-\DataMovementEnergyFraction)\) reduction factor when the remaining terms are unchanged. For an illustrative 60–80 percent share, that bound is 2.5–5\(\times\), before accounting for replacement circuitry.

Henderson, Peter, Jieru Hu, Joshua Romoff, Emma Brunskill, Dan Jurafsky, and Joelle Pineau. 2020. “Towards the Systematic Reporting of the Energy and Carbon Footprints of Machine Learning.” CoRR abs/2002.05651 (248): 1–43. https://doi.org/10.48550/arxiv.2002.05651.

A critical implementation barrier is the “measurement gap”: AI teams need standardized, workload-level reporting of energy use and carbon emissions rather than relying only on coarse proxy metrics (Henderson et al. 2020). Coarse methods often rely on proxies such as GPU-hours multiplied by average grid intensity, which fail to capture the real-world dynamics required by reporting regimes that care about time, location, and workload boundary. Developing and standardizing granular, real-time energy and carbon accounting tools is essential for both compliance and effective optimization.

A second research direction reduces redundant computation before it reaches the accelerator. Data selection, model design, and training procedure all affect the environmental cost of learning (Wu et al. 2022). Smarter data sampling, active learning, and data valuation can reduce work spent on low-value examples, but the resulting model quality and coverage must be measured rather than assumed. An integrated approach combining algorithmic efficiency, hardware innovation, renewable energy adoption, and transparent governance is necessary to align AI development with environmental constraints.

Wu, Carole-Jean, Ramya Raghavendra, Udit Gupta, Bilge Acun, Newsha Ardalani, Kiwan Maeng, Gloria Chang, et al. 2022. “Sustainable AI: Environmental Implications, Challenges and Opportunities.” Proceedings of Machine Learning and Systems 4: 795–813.

Reducing redundant computation can improve operational efficiency, but compliance and environmental benefit require their own evidence. A sustainable design must show that the saved work survives the complete lifecycle account and the resulting deployment scale. Incorrect boundaries or assumptions can otherwise turn a local improvement into a larger total footprint.

Self-Check: Question

  1. An industry observer claims that policy interventions and carbon pricing are unnecessary because ‘free-market competition will naturally incentivize data center operators to minimize energy use and carbon emissions.’ Which counterargument best explains why market mechanisms alone fail to guarantee sustainable AI deployment?

    1. Data center operators are legally restricted from purchasing power from renewable energy utilities.
    2. Electricity prices can omit emissions and other environmental burdens, so minimizing the electricity bill need not minimize the complete environmental account; pricing, reporting, or procurement requirements can change the decision incentives.
    3. AI models running on renewable electricity automatically suffer from higher inference latency than models on fossil power.
    4. Hardware vendors have already achieved the physical thermodynamic limit of computing efficiency, leaving no room for further optimization.

  2. How do modern regulatory frameworks such as the Corporate Sustainability Reporting Directive (CSRD) and the EU AI Act directly affect the systems engineering practices of organizations deploying large-scale AI?

    1. They impose an absolute global cap banning the training of any machine learning model exceeding 100 billion parameters.
    2. For organizations and systems within their applicable scope, they create documentation and disclosure duties that require traceable energy and sustainability evidence; the exact obligations depend on the framework and deployment.
    3. They mandate that all AI inference must be executed exclusively on battery-powered mobile devices rather than cloud servers.
    4. They require that all machine learning models be retrained weekly using uncompressed full-precision floating-point arithmetic.

  3. Explain how an applicable emissions trading system or carbon tax changes a workload scheduler’s cost objective, and what conditions determine whether cleaner placement becomes economically preferable.

  4. True or False: If a technology enterprise purchases enough annual Renewable Energy Certificates (RECs) to match 100 percent of its data centers’ annual electricity consumption, its evening foundation-model inference workloads running on a fossil-fueled regional grid can be accurately characterized as zero-carbon operations.

  5. Explain how future non-von-Neumann computing paradigms (such as neuromorphic computing and in-memory analog computing) address the fundamental physical bottleneck responsible for AI energy consumption.
